//  stack:  r ───────►  heap: [ length | breadth ]
  • Examples from other engineering fieldsa car (colour, speed / start(), stop()), a television (channel, volume / turnOn()), a building plan (blueprint) vs the actual building
  • newallocates the object in heap and calls the constructor; the statement result is a reference stored in the variable
  • Separate .classevery class produces its own .class file
Local variables and references live in the stack; objects are always created in the heap.
How to Write a Class

Decide the properties (data, with their types) and the methods (actions). Identify them from the requirement — a generic class would include *all possible* properties/methods; start with what you need.

Class + application class (two classes in a file; only one may be public)class Rectangle {
    double length, breadth;                       // properties (instance variables)

    double area()      { return length * breadth; }
    double perimeter() { return 2 * (length + breadth); }
    boolean isSquare() { return length == breadth; }        // simply return the condition
}
public class RectangleTest {                      // application class – has main()
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Rectangle r1 = new Rectangle();           // create object
        r1.length = 10;  r1.breadth = 5;          // access with the dot (.)
        Rectangle r2 = new Rectangle();
        r2.length = 4;   r2.breadth = 4;
        System.out.println(r1.area() + "  " + r2.isSquare());   // 50.0  true
    }
}
  • Dot operatorobject.property / object.method() — the same method behaves according to the object it is called on (c1.area() uses c1's radius)
  • Design examplesStudent → rollNo (int), name (String), marks (int) + total(), grade(); Circle → radius; Cylinder → radius, height
  • isSquare() stylemethod starting with is returning boolean = an inquiry method
Circle, Rectangle, Cylinder & Student — Student Challenges 11.4 – 11.7
Circleclass Circle {
    double radius;
    double area()          { return Math.PI * radius * radius; }
    double circumference() { return 2 * Math.PI * radius; }    // perimeter
}
Cylinder  (11.6) – lid area, total surface area, volumeclass Cylinder {
    double radius, height;
    double lidArea()   { return Math.PI * radius * radius; }
    double totalSurfaceArea() { return 2 * lidArea() + 2 * Math.PI * radius * height; }
    double volume()    { return lidArea() * height; }
}
Student  (11.7) – total, grade (A / B) and toString()class Student {
    int rollNo;  String name;  int m1, m2, m3;
    int total()        { return m1 + m2 + m3; }
    double average()   { return total() / 3.0; }
    char grade()       { return average() >= 60 ? 'A' : 'B'; }
    @Override public String toString() { return rollNo + " " + name + " " + total() + " " + grade(); }
}
// System.out.println(s);  ← calls s.toString() automatically
Rectangle (11.5): add perimeter() and isSquare() (returns length == breadth directly — no if/else needed). Cylinder: reduce digits using printf("%.2f").
If you print an object directly (System.out.println(s)) you get Student@1b6d3586 unless the class implements toString().
Data Hiding (Encapsulation)

Make properties private so outside code can't touch them directly; give controlled access through public getter/setter methods that can validate.

class Rectangle {
    private double length, breadth;                 // hidden

    public double getLength()          { return length; }
    public void   setLength(double l)  { length = (l >= 0) ? l : 0; }     // validation: never negative
    public double getBreadth()         { return breadth; }
    public void   setBreadth(double b) { breadth = (b >= 0) ? b : 0; }
    public double area() { return length * breadth; }
}
Rectangle r = new Rectangle();
// r.length = 10;         // ✘ error: length has private access in Rectangle
r.setLength(10);          // ✔ through public method
r.setBreadth(-5);         // stored as 0 – setter protects the data
  • Whyprevent invalid values (negative length), allow changing internal design without breaking users, security
  • Namingproperty radius → getRadius() / setRadius(); first word lowercase, each following word capitalised (setBreadth)
  • Boolean getterisSquare() / isEmpty()
  • Default valuean unset numeric property is 0
  • Rule of thumbproperties private, methods public
Just like you press a button without touching the internal circuit — users call methods, not fields.
Types of Properties — Read/Write, Read-Only, Write-Only
TypeMethods providedExample
Read-Writegetter + setterStudent name, Rectangle length
Read-Onlyonly getterstudent rollNo (given once at creation), product itemNumber, item name, account balance
Write-Onlyonly settera charity account you can only donate into; a producer/password field you can set but never read back
class Student {
    private final int rollNo;                        // read-only: set in constructor, no setter
    private String name;                             // read-write
    private String password;                         // write-only
    Student(int rollNo) { this.rollNo = rollNo; }
    public int    getRollNo()  { return rollNo; }
    public String getName()    { return name; }
    public void   setName(String n) { name = n; }
    public void   setPassword(String p) { password = p; }   // no getter
}
  • Decision ruleask *"Can this value change after the object exists? Should outsiders be able to read it?"*
Constructors

A special method invoked automatically when an object is created — used to initialise the properties.

  • Namesame as the class name
  • Return typenone (not even void). If you give a return type it becomes an ordinary method
  • Default constructorif you write no constructor, the compiler supplies a no-arg default constructor (sets defaults 0/null/false). The moment you write any constructor, the default one disappears
  • Overloadingseveral constructors with different parameter lists are allowed
  • Called bynew ClassName(args)
class Rectangle {
    private double length, breadth;

    Rectangle()                        { length = 1; breadth = 1; }          // non-parameterised (default values 1)
    Rectangle(double s)                { setLength(s); setBreadth(s); }      // one parameter → square with all sides = s
    Rectangle(double l, double b)      { setLength(l); setBreadth(b); }      // parameterised
    // … getters/setters …
}
Rectangle a = new Rectangle();          // 1 × 1
Rectangle b = new Rectangle(10);        // 10 × 10  (all four sides 10)
Rectangle c = new Rectangle(10, 5);
If a class only has Rectangle(double l, double b) and you write new Rectangle() → compile error (*constructor cannot be applied*). Provide a no-arg one when you need it.
Constructors may call setters so validation is applied at creation too.
this Keyword
  • Meaningreference to the current object (the one whose method/constructor is running)
  • Resolve name clashwhen parameter and field have the same name → this.field = param
  • Call another constructorthis(args) must be the first statement (constructor chaining)
class Subject {
    private int subId;
    private String title;
    Subject(int subId)            { this.subId = subId; }           // this.subId = field, subId = parameter
    Subject(int subId, String t)  { this(subId); this.title = t; }  // calls the first constructor
}
Student Challenges 11.13 & 11.14 — Cylinder Properties & Product / Customer
11.13 Cylinder with private radius, height; getters/setters (getRadius() — capital R after get, though the property is radius); constructors; methods.
11.14 Product & Customer — decide for each property whether it is read-write, read-only or write-only.
class Product {
    private final int itemNumber;      // must be given at creation, never changes → NO setter
    private final String name;         // a Pepsi can stays "Pepsi" → NO setName()
    private double price;              // can change → getter + setter
    Product(int itemNumber, String name) { this.itemNumber = itemNumber; this.name = name; }   // NO no-arg constructor
    Product(int itemNumber, String name, double price) { this(itemNumber, name); setPrice(price); }
    public int getItemNumber() { return itemNumber; }
    public String getName()    { return name; }
    public double getPrice()   { return price; }
    public void setPrice(double p) { price = p > 0 ? p : 0; }
}
class Customer {
    private final int id;  private String name, address;     // name & address compulsory → constructor arguments
    Customer(int id, String name, String address) { this.id = id; this.name = name; this.address = address; }
    // getters + setters for name & address, getter only for id
}
  • Rule learnedsome properties must have only get (no set); mandatory values are demanded in the constructor and there is no non-parameterised constructor
Array of Objects — Challenge 11.15 (Subject & Student)

An array of objects stores references; each slot is null until you create the object.

class Subject {
    private int subId;  private String name;
    Subject(int subId) { this.subId = subId; }          // subject ID compulsory → no default constructor
    public int getSubId() { return subId; }
    public void setName(String name) { this.name = name; }
    public String toString() { return subId + " " + name; }
}
class Student {
    private int rollNo;
    private Subject[] subjects = new Subject[3];        // array of Subject references (all null)
    Student(int rollNo) { this.rollNo = rollNo; }
    public void setSubjects(Subject[] s) { subjects = s; }   // subjects are chosen during the course → setter needed
}
public class Test {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Subject[] arr = new Subject[3];
        for (int i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) arr[i] = new Subject(100 + i);   // must create EACH object
        arr[0].setName("Java");
        Student s = new Student(1);
        s.setSubjects(arr);
    }
}
new Subject[3] creates only the array, not the 3 Subject objects — calling a method on an element before creating it gives NullPointerException.
12

Inheritance, super, Overriding & Dynamic Method Dispatch

Lectures 12.1 – 12.15
Generalization vs Specialization
GeneralizationSpecialization
Directionbottom-up — find common parts of existing classes and write a superclasstop-down — take an existing class and derive a more specific subclass
ExampleCar, Bike, Truck → VehicleVehicle → Car → SportsCar
Java toolinterfaces (pure generalization)inheritance (extends)
In betweenabstract classes — partly general, partly implemented
  • Parent namessuperclass = base class = parent · subclass = derived class = child
  • Think of it as "is-a"SmartTV *is a* TV; Cylinder *is a* Circle (properly speaking, design-wise ask yourself if it is truly "is-a")
What Is Inheritance?

Inheritance is the process of acquiring the features (properties + methods) of an existing class into a new class. Keyword: extends.

class Circle {
    protected double radius;                       // or public/private + getters
    public double area() { return Math.PI * radius * radius; }
    public double circumference() { return 2 * Math.PI * radius; }
}
class Cylinder extends Circle {                    // Cylinder HAS everything Circle has
    double height;
    double volume() { return area() * height; }    // reuses parent's area()
}
Cylinder c1 = new Cylinder();
c1.radius = 5; c1.height = 10;
System.out.println(c1.area());       // inherited method
System.out.println(c1.volume());     // own method
  • Reusewrite common code once in the parent, use it in all children
  • Only public/protected (and package) members are accessible in the child — private ones are inherited but not directly accessible
  • Java allows one direct parent only (no multiple inheritance of classes); a class can have many children; chains are allowed (A → B → C)
  • Rootevery class implicitly extends java.lang.Object
TypeShapeJava
SingleA → B✔
MultilevelA → B → C✔
HierarchicalA → B, A → C✔
Multiple (two parents)A, B → C✘ classes / ✔ through interfaces
Student Challenge 12.4 — Account & Customer (brainstorm inheritance)
A brainstorming challenge: find what is common and what is special, then write the hierarchy. Examples discussed: (1) Account → SavingsAccount, LoanAccount (a loan needs a repayment method) · (2) a CellPhone class hierarchy — you can't write a real phone, only a *replica*: focus on the methods (call(), playMusic() → just print "Music is playing") and show how a SmartPhone extends it.
class Account {
    protected int accNo;  protected double balance;
    void deposit(double a)  { balance += a; }
    void withdraw(double a) { if (a <= balance) balance -= a; }
}
class LoanAccount extends Account {
    double loanAmount;
    void repay(double a) { loanAmount -= a; }              // extra behaviour
}
class CellPhone { void call() { System.out.println("Calling..."); }  void playMusic() { System.out.println("Music is playing"); } }
class SmartPhone extends CellPhone { void browse() { System.out.println("Browsing"); } }
Constructors in Inheritance
  • Ordercreating a child object runs the parent constructor first, then the child constructor (base object is built, then extended). Real example: Car → Hatchback → Swift → SwiftDzire: constructors run top-down
  • Implicitevery child constructor secretly starts with super(); (calls parent's no-arg constructor)
class Parent { Parent() { System.out.println("Parent constructor"); } }
class Child extends Parent { Child() { System.out.println("Child constructor"); } }
new Child();
OutputParent constructor
Child constructor
If the parent has only parameterised constructors, the child's implicit super() fails: *constructor Parent in class Parent cannot be applied*. Call super(args) yourself.
Parameterised Constructor & super(...)

To pass values to the parent's constructor use super(arguments) — it must be the first statement in the child constructor.

class Parent {
    int x;
    Parent(int x) { this.x = x; System.out.println("Parent " + x); }
}
class Child extends Parent {
    int y;
    Child(int x, int y) {
        super(x);                 // ✔ first line; sends 10 to Parent
        this.y = y;               // 20 stays in Child
        // super(x);              // ✘ error if written after another statement
    }
}
new Child(10, 20);                // 10 → parent's x, 20 → child's y
Rectangle → Cuboidclass Rectangle { double l, b; Rectangle(double l, double b) { this.l = l; this.b = b; } }
class Cuboid extends Rectangle {
    double h;
    Cuboid(double l, double b, double h) { super(l, b); this.h = h; }
    double volume() { return l * b * h; }
}
this vs super
thissuper
Refers tothe current objectthe parent-class part of the current object
Fieldthis.length = l (fixes name clash with parameter)super.length (parent's field when child hides it)
Methodthis.show()super.show() — call parent's overridden version
Constructorthis(args) → another constructor of the same classsuper(args) → constructor of the parent class
Positionfirst statement in constructorfirst statement in constructor
Both together–cannot use this(...) and super(...) in the same constructor (both must be first)
class Rectangle {
    double length, breadth;
    Rectangle(double length, double breadth) {
        this.length = length;         // this.length = field ; length = parameter
        this.breadth = breadth;       // "this" = the object on which the constructor is called
    }
}
class Cuboid extends Rectangle {
    double height;
    Cuboid(double l, double b, double h) { super(l, b); this.height = h; }
    void show() { System.out.println(super.length + " " + this.height); }
}
  • Importantthis covers the whole object (own + inherited); super points only to the portion coming from the parent class
  • Static contextneither can be used inside a static method
Method Overriding

A subclass provides its own version of a method that the superclass already has — same name, same parameters (signature), compatible return type.

class Super { void display() { System.out.println("Hello welcome (super)"); } }
class Sub extends Super {
    @Override                                         // annotation → compiler checks you really override
    void display() { System.out.println("Sub display"); }
    void extra() { }
}
Sub s = new Sub();
s.display();                    // Sub display  – parent's version is HIDDEN for Sub objects
  • Golden rulethe method called depends on the OBJECT (created at runtime), not on the reference type
  • @Overrideoptional but strongly recommended; IDEs suggest it; compile error if there is no matching parent method (typo protection)
  • Call parent's versionsuper.display() inside the child method
  • Subclass-only methodscan't be called through a parent reference
Example 1 – Television → SmartTVclass Television  { void switchOn() { System.out.println("Press power button"); }  void changeChannel() {} }
class SmartTV extends Television {
    @Override void switchOn() { System.out.println("Voice command / app start"); }  // new way of switching on
    void openApp() { }                        // new method (only in SmartTV)
}
new SmartTV().switchOn();                     // SmartTV's version
Example 2 – Car → LuxuryCar (override changeGear)class Car { void changeGear() { System.out.println("Manual gear"); }  void start() {} void stop() {} }
class LuxuryCar extends Car {
    @Override void changeGear() { System.out.println("Automatic gear"); }
    void openRoof() { System.out.println("Roof opened"); }
}
LuxuryCar c = new LuxuryCar();
c.changeGear();  c.openRoof();
Design in software is done with object orientation like in other engineering: define a general product, then customise behaviour in each specialised model by overriding.
Dynamic Method Dispatch (Runtime Polymorphism)

A superclass reference holding a subclass object and calling an overridden method → the method of the OBJECT runs. "Dispatch" = calling; decided at runtime.

class Super { void meth1() { print("Super meth1"); }  void meth2() { print("Super meth2"); } }
class Sub extends Super {
    void meth2() { print("Sub meth2"); }            // overridden
    void meth3() { print("Sub meth3"); }            // new
}
Super ref = new Sub();          // superclass reference → subclass object (upcasting, automatic)
ref.meth1();                    // Super meth1   (only one version exists)
ref.meth2();                    // Sub meth2     ← object's version is called
// ref.meth3();                 // ✘ cannot find symbol – reference knows only Super's methods
((Sub) ref).meth3();            // ✔ downcast, then call
// Sub s = new Super();         // ✘ Super cannot be converted to Sub
ConceptDecided byWhen
What methods you may callthe reference typecompile time
Which version of an overridden method runsthe actual objectrun time
  • Reference of superclassknows only the methods present in the superclass
  • Usewrite general code once, works with every present and future subclass
Shape[] shapes = { new Circle(), new Rectangle(), new Triangle() };
for (Shape s : shapes) System.out.println(s.area());     // each object computes its own area
Dos and Don'ts of Overriding
RuleDetail
Signaturename and parameter list exactly the same (otherwise it's overloading)
Return typemust be same; only exception: covariant return — may be a subclass of the parent's return type (A get() → B get() where B extends A). Not allowed for primitives
Access modifiermay increase, never reduce visibility — parent public → child must be public; parent protected → child protected or public; private ✘ error
final method✘ cannot be overridden
static method✘ cannot be overridden (a same-name static in child *hides* it)
private methodnot inherited → not overridden
Constructorsnot inherited → cannot be overridden
Checked exceptionschild can't throw broader checked exceptions than parent
Fieldsnot overridden — field access depends on the reference type
class A {} class B extends A {}
class P { public A get() { return new A(); } }
class C extends P { @Override public B get() { return new B(); } }   // ✔ covariant return
class Q { public void display() {} }
// class R extends Q { void display() {} }        // ✘ weaker access (package-private < public)
Polymorphism — Overloading vs Overriding

Poly = many, morphism = forms → one name, different actions.

Method OverloadingMethod Overriding
Classsame class (or subclass)needs inheritance (two classes)
Signaturemust differ (number/type/order of parameters)must be identical
Return typemay differsame / covariant
Resolvedcompile time (by arguments) → *static / early binding*run time (by object) → *dynamic / late binding*
Polymorphism kindcompile-time polymorphismruntime polymorphism (dynamic method dispatch)
Private/static/finalallowednot allowed
class Print {
    void show(int x) { }  void show(String s) { }           // overloading – same name, different parameters
}
class Animal { void sound() { System.out.println("..."); } }
class Dog extends Animal { void sound() { System.out.println("Bow bow"); } }   // overriding
Animal a = new Dog();  a.sound();                            // runtime decision → Bow bow
The overridden method is chosen when the program runs because objects are created at runtime; the overloaded method is chosen when the program compiles.
Deep Dive — Choosing: Generalization vs Specialization (lecture examples)

The instructor gives several pairs so you can *think* object-oriented. Both draw a hierarchy (one thing on top, things coming out of it) — the only difference is which came first.

ExampleTop (general)Below (specific)How it was born
VehicleVehicle = something a human controls that can moveCar, Bike, Truckclasses existed → grouped them (generalization, bottom-up)
ShapeShape = has dimensions, area, perimeterCircle, Rectangle, Trianglecommon parts pulled up into a parent
Circle → CylinderCircle (radius, area)Cylinder (+ height, volume)Circle already perfect; derived a new class adding features (specialization, top-down)
Guitar → Electric guitarGuitarElectric guitarspecialization
Phone → Smartphonemobile / cell phone (first)smartphone (came *later* with extra features)the term appeared after — classic specialization
  • Generalizationyou already have Car, Bike, Truck → find common properties → write a (possibly *dummy*) superclass so you can refer to all of them by one name
  • Specializationyou already have a perfect class → derive a subclass, borrow everything, add a few extra features
  • Java toolsinterfaces → generalization · inheritance (extends) → specialization · abstract classes → something *in between* the two (partly general, partly implemented)
  • IS-A test"Smartphone is a Phone" ✔ → inheritance. "Store has a list of Members" → containment (an object as a field). *is-a = inheritance; has-a = containment (having an object)*
RelationshipMeaningJavaExample
IS-Ainheritanceclass B extends ASmartTV is a TV
HAS-Acontainment / compositionfield of another class typeCar has an Engine; app class has a Frame
Prefer has-a (composition) when the "is-a" sentence sounds forced (a Stack is not really a Vector). Use inheritance only for a true specialization.
Deep Dive — What Does a Subclass Object Contain?

The word to remember is "having": when you create a Cylinder, the object has everything from Circle — the Circle constructor, its property, its methods — plus its own extras.

class Circle {                                   // parent
    double radius;
    Circle()               { System.out.println("Circle constructor"); }
    double area()          { return Math.PI * radius * radius; }
    double circumference() { return 2 * Math.PI * radius; }
}
class Cylinder extends Circle {                  // child: "extends" = acquires everything of Circle
    double height;                               // only ONE extra property
    Cylinder()             { System.out.println("Cylinder constructor"); }
    double volume()        { return area() * height; }     // uses the inherited area()
}
Cylinder c1 = new Cylinder();      // Circle constructor  →  Cylinder constructor
c1.radius = 5;  c1.height = 10;    // radius is inherited (public/default here)
System.out.println(c1.area() + " " + c1.volume());
Memory picture (heap)c1 ─►  ┌──────────────── Cylinder object ────────────────┐
       │  portion from Circle  →  radius , area() , circumference() ← this part is what `super` points to
       │  portion of Cylinder  →  height , volume()
       └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  • Inheritance chainCylinder → Circle → Object (every class ends at java.lang.Object)
  • Private membersare part of the object but not directly accessible in the child — use protected/public or getters
  • Only public/protected/package members are accessible in the child; a child can also define its own property with the same name (hides parent's)
Deep Dive — Constructor Chain: Parent → Child → Grandchild

A subclass object is built from the base up, exactly like a factory builds a car: first the platform, then the car on it. Lecture example: Hatchback (platform) → Swift → Swift Dzire — to construct a Dzire the Swift must be constructed first, and before that the hatchback platform.

class Parent     { Parent()     { System.out.println("Parent constructor"); } }
class Child      extends Parent { Child()      { System.out.println("Child constructor"); } }
class GrandChild extends Child  { GrandChild() { System.out.println("Grandchild constructor"); } }
new GrandChild();
OutputParent constructor
Child constructor
Grandchild constructor
1

new GrandChild()

JVM allocates the whole object in the heap and calls GrandChild's constructor

2

First statement of every constructor

an invisible super(); is inserted by the compiler (unless you wrote this(...) / super(...) yourself)

3

Chain runs upward

GrandChild → Child → Parent → Object constructors are *entered* in that order

4

Bodies complete downward

Object → Parent → Child → GrandChild — that is the order of the printed messages

Parameterised constructors — you must pass the values up yourselfclass Parent { Parent(int x)           { System.out.println("Parent " + x); }   Parent() { } }
class Child extends Parent {
    Child()              { }                              // implicit super() → Parent() ✔ (exists)
    Child(int x, int y)  { super(x);  System.out.println("Child " + y); }   // 10 → Parent, 20 → Child
}
new Child(10, 20);
  • If Parent has only Parent(int) and Child has no super(...) call → compile error (implicit super() finds nothing)
  • Rectangle → CuboidCuboid takes three values but keeps only *height*; length & breadth go to Rectangle via super(l, b)
  • Rulethe call to super(...) must be the very first line — writing it after another statement is an error
Deep Dive — Overriding: the Philosophy & the Hidden Method

Always remember: the method is called depending on the OBJECT, not on the reference. That is the one sentence behind overriding, dynamic dispatch and polymorphism.

class Super { void display() { System.out.println("Hello, welcome (Super)"); } }
class Sub extends Super { void display() { System.out.println("Sub display"); } }   // redefining an inherited method

Sub s1 = new Sub();     s1.display();      // Sub display   – Super's version is SHADOWED (hidden) for Sub objects
Super s2 = new Sub();   s2.display();      // Sub display   – reference is Super, OBJECT is Sub → object's method
Super s3 = new Super(); s3.display();      // Hello, welcome (Super)
  • Shadowinga Sub object contains both display() versions, but the parent's is hidden; super.display() inside Sub reaches it
  • Real-life logica smart TV stays a smart TV even if you casually call it "a TV". *You* look at it through a plain-TV lens (the reference) but the thing itself decides how it switches on (the object)
Television → SmartTV  (Example 1)class Television  { void switchOn() { print("press power button"); }   void changeChannel() { print("next channel"); } }
class SmartTV extends Television {
    @Override void switchOn() { print("voice / app start"); }          // overridden – new way to switch on
    void browse()            { print("browsing internet"); }            // NEW method, only in SmartTV
}
SmartTV t1 = new SmartTV();  t1.switchOn();  t1.browse();              // all three available
Television t2 = new SmartTV();
t2.switchOn();                 // "voice / app start" – object's version
// t2.browse();                // ✘ cannot find symbol – "you think it's an ordinary TV and don't know browsing exists"
((SmartTV) t2).browse();       // ✔ tell the compiler you know it is a SmartTV
Car → LuxuryCar (Example 2) — start(), accelerate() inherited; changeGear() overridden; openRoof() newclass Car       { void start() {…}  void accelerate() {…}  void changeGear() { print("manual gear"); } }
class LuxuryCar extends Car { @Override void changeGear() { print("automatic gear"); }  void openRoof() { print("roof opened"); } }
LuxuryCar c = new LuxuryCar();
c.start(); c.accelerate();     // coming from Car
c.changeGear();                // LuxuryCar's version
c.openRoof();
Deep Dive — References: What Can Hold What?
StatementLegal?Why
Super s = new Sub();✔ upcasting (automatic)a Sub is-a Super
Sub b = new Super();✘ *incompatible types: Super cannot be converted to Sub*the object lacks Sub's extra members — a *smart* reference can't hold a *basic* object
Sub b = (Sub) superRefHoldingSub;✔ downcasting (explicit)object really is a Sub
Sub b = (Sub) new Super();compiles, ❌ ClassCastException at run timeobject isn't a Sub
Safe downcastSuper s = getSomething();
if (s instanceof Sub) {                      // check the OBJECT type first
    Sub b = (Sub) s;   b.subOnlyMethod();
}
if (s instanceof Sub b2) b2.subOnlyMethod();   // Java 16+ pattern matching – no separate cast
  • Reference type decides *what you may call* (compile time); object type decides *which overridden version runs* (run time)
  • Superclass reference knows only the methods of the superclass — subclass-only methods are invisible through it
  • Why this mattersthe same code Shape s; s.area(); works for every existing and future subclass — the basis of frameworks
Deep Dive — Traps & Advanced Points in Inheritance
TrapExplanation
Fields are not polymorphicSuper s = new Sub(); s.x reads Super's x even if Sub declares its own x — only methods are overridden
static methods are hidden, not overriddencall resolves by reference type
Overridden method called from parent constructorthe Sub version runs *before* Sub's fields are initialised → may see 0/null
private parent methodinvisible to child; same-named child method is a new method
final method / classcan't be overridden / extended (String, Math)
Constructorsnever inherited — only *called* via super(...)
protected access in another packageonly through inheritance, not through a parent-type reference
Multiple inheritance of classes✘ — use interfaces (see Interfaces section)
Overridden method in a parent constructor — a classic bugclass Base {
    Base() { show(); }                          // calls the OVERRIDDEN version while Derived is half-built
    void show() { System.out.println("Base"); }
}
class Derived extends Base {
    int v = 10;
    Derived() { super(); }
    @Override void show() { System.out.println("Derived v=" + v); }   // prints v=0 !
}
new Derived();      // Derived v=0
Field hiding vs method overridingclass P { int x = 1; void m() { print("P.m"); } }
class C extends P { int x = 2; void m() { print("C.m"); } }
P p = new C();
System.out.println(p.x);   // 1  ← field decided by REFERENCE type
p.m();                      // C.m ← method decided by OBJECT
Practice — Inheritance & Polymorphism Questions
QuestionAnswer
What is inheritance?acquiring the properties and methods of an existing class into a new class (extends)
Which constructor runs first when a child object is created?the parent's; then the child's (top-down)
How do you call a parent's parameterised constructor?super(args) as the first statement of the child constructor
Difference between this and super?this = current object (whole), super = parent-class portion of it; both can call constructors, but not in the same constructor
Overloading vs overriding?overloading: same class/different parameters, compile time · overriding: parent-child/same signature, run time
Can a static method be overridden?No — hidden, resolved at compile time by the reference type
Can you override a private / final method?No
What is dynamic method dispatch?superclass reference holding a subclass object calls an overridden method → the object's method runs at run time
Can Sub b = new Super(); compile?No; Super s = new Sub(); is fine
Can a child reduce access of an overridden method?No — public in parent ⇒ public in child
Can Java classes inherit from many classes?No; one class only, many interfaces
Which class is the parent of all classes?java.lang.Object
Predict the output: class A { A(){ print("A"); } } class B extends A { B(){ print("B"); } B(int x){ this(); print("Bx"); } } class C extends B { C(){ super(5); print("C"); } } new C(); → prints A B Bx C.
13

Abstract Classes

Lectures 13.1 – 13.7
What Is an Abstract Class?

A class that is not completely defined → its object cannot be created. Declared with the keyword abstract. It exists to be extended.

abstract class Super {
    Super() { System.out.println("Super constructor"); }     // constructors allowed (called via subclass)
    void meth1() { System.out.println("concrete method"); }  // concrete = has body
    abstract void meth2();                                    // abstract = NO body, ends with ;
}
class Sub extends Super {                                     // concrete class
    void meth2() { System.out.println("Sub meth2"); }         // MUST override every abstract method
}
// Super s = new Super();          // ✘ error: Super is abstract; cannot be instantiated
Super s = new Sub();               // ✔ reference of abstract class, object of concrete subclass
s.meth2();                         // Sub meth2  (dynamic method dispatch)
  • Abstract methoddeclaration only — abstract void area(); — the subclass supplies the body
  • Concrete classevery method defined; objects can be created
  • Rule of inheritancea subclass of an abstract class must override all abstract methods to become concrete; otherwise it must itself be declared abstract
  • Referenceyou can declare a reference of an abstract type (used for polymorphism) but cannot create an object of it
  • Can containfields, constructors, concrete methods, static methods, abstract methods, main
  • Purpose(1) impose a standard / contract on subclasses, (2) share common code, (3) achieve polymorphism
If a class has even one abstract method, the class must be declared abstract.
Real-Life Examples — Abstract Classes Impose Standards
Example 1 – Hospitalabstract class Hospital {                      // the "government standard"
    void register() { System.out.println("Common registration procedure"); }   // concrete – same everywhere
    abstract void appointment();               // each hospital must define its own way
}
class CityCare extends Hospital {              // only if you inherit from Hospital will people call it a hospital
    @Override void appointment() { System.out.println("Online + phone booking"); }
}
Example 2 – KFC franchiseabstract class KFC {
    KFC() { System.out.println("KFC brand standards applied"); }           // concrete constructor
    void makeItem() { System.out.println("Recipe as defined by KFC"); }    // concrete – KFC gives the exact way
    abstract void localMenu();                                              // franchise decides the local dishes
}
class KFCIndore extends KFC { void localMenu() { System.out.println("Spicy veg zinger"); } }
  • Ideathe abstract class is the rulebook; concrete subclasses implement the rules — the subclass can't skip a rule without also being abstract
  • Points to rememberobject of abstract class ✘ · reference of abstract class ✔ · subclass must implement abstract methods ✔
Student Challenge 13.5 — Class Shape
Write an abstract class Shape with abstract area() and perimeter(). Circle and Rectangle extend it and must override both. Test using a Shape reference pointing at Rectangle 10 × 5 → 50 (dynamic method dispatch).
abstract class Shape {
    abstract double area();
    abstract double perimeter();
}
class Circle extends Shape {
    double r;  Circle(double r) { this.r = r; }
    double area()      { return Math.PI * r * r; }
    double perimeter() { return 2 * Math.PI * r; }
}
class Rectangle extends Shape {
    double l, b;  Rectangle(double l, double b) { this.l = l; this.b = b; }
    double area()      { return l * b; }
    double perimeter() { return 2 * (l + b); }
}
public class ShapeTest {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Rectangle r = new Rectangle(10, 5);
        System.out.println(r.area());                // 50.0
        Shape s = r;                                 // superclass reference
        System.out.println(s.area());                // 50.0 – object's method
        s = new Circle(7);
        System.out.println(s.area());                // Circle's area – polymorphism
    }
}
Rules — Dos & Don'ts of Abstract Classes / Methods
RuleReason
Cannot create object of an abstract classincomplete definition
A class with ≥ 1 abstract method must be abstractotherwise it would have undefined methods
An abstract class can have zero abstract methodsstill can't be instantiated (used to block object creation)
Abstract class cannot be finalfinal = cannot be extended, abstract = must be extended → contradiction
Abstract method cannot be finalfinal = cannot be overridden, abstract = must be overridden
Abstract method cannot be staticstatic methods need a body and can't be overridden
Abstract method cannot be privateprivate can't be seen by the subclass to override
Subclass must override abstract methodselse the subclass must be declared abstract too
Abstract class can have constructorscalled through super() from subclass constructors
abstract final class A {}                 // ✘ illegal combination of modifiers
abstract class B { abstract final void m(); }     // ✘
abstract class C { abstract static void m(); }    // ✘
abstract class D { abstract void m(); }           // ✔
class E extends D { void m() { } }                // ✔ concrete (must override)
abstract ⟷ needs subclass · final ⟷ forbids subclass/override — they never go together. (Difference between static and final comes in the Static / Final members section.)
Deep Dive — Abstract Class Anatomy & Why It Exists

Abstract classes let you impose a standard: "if you want to be called a *hospital / KFC / shape*, you must inherit from me and implement what I left undefined." That is the core reason they exist — standards + polymorphism + code sharing.

abstract class  ──►  can have:  constructors · concrete (defined) methods · abstract (undefined) methods · fields · static members
                     cannot:    be instantiated (no object)   ·   can:  have a REFERENCE
concrete subclass ─►  must override EVERY abstract method  (or it must be declared abstract too)
1

Abstract class defines the standard

some things fully implemented (common to all), some declared abstract (each subclass differs)

2

A subclass inherits from it

"only if you inherit from Hospital will people call your class a hospital"

3

Subclass overrides all abstract methods

becomes concrete → objects can be created

4

Use through the abstract reference

Hospital h = new CityCare(); h.emergency(); — polymorphism

Example 1 — Hospital: the government's standardabstract class Hospital {
    void register() { System.out.println("Common registration procedure"); }   // concrete – same for all
    abstract void emergency();                 // how emergencies are handled – each hospital decides
    abstract void appointment();               // how appointments are given
}
class CityCare extends Hospital {              // if it doesn't override BOTH it must be abstract
    void emergency()   { System.out.println("24x7 trauma unit"); }
    void appointment() { System.out.println("online booking"); }
}
// new Hospital();   ✘  – you cannot find "a hospital" in the abstract; only real hospitals exist
Hospital h = new CityCare();                   // ✔ reference of abstract class
Example 2 — KFC franchise (template): concrete parts + parts the franchisee must supplyabstract class KFC {
    KFC() { System.out.println("KFC standards applied"); }          // concrete constructor – you get it with the franchise
    void makeItem() { System.out.println("Recipe exactly as defined by KFC"); }   // concrete: HOW to make each dish is fixed by KFC
    abstract void billing();                                         // franchisee must build own billing system
    abstract void offers();                                          // …and run own offers for customers
}
class MyKFC extends KFC {                                            // not abstract → must implement billing() and offers()
    void billing() { System.out.println("my POS billing"); }
    void offers()  { System.out.println("festival discounts"); }
}
new MyKFC().makeItem();
Same idea as a "template method" (very common in frameworks)abstract class Report {
    final void generate() { load(); format(); print(); }     // fixed algorithm (final = cannot change the steps)
    abstract void load();  abstract void format();           // steps subclasses fill in
    void print() { System.out.println("printing…"); }        // default step
}
  • Facts from the lecture examples① object of abstract class ✘ · ② reference of abstract class ✔ · ③ subclass must implement all abstract methods to be concrete · ④ subclass of an abstract class is itself abstract until it overrides everything
  • Partial implementationan abstract class may implement *some* abstract methods of its parent and leave the rest for the next subclass:
abstract class A { abstract void x(); abstract void y(); }
abstract class B extends A { void x() { } }          // still abstract – y() pending
class C extends B { void y() { } }                   // concrete – both defined now
Deep Dive — Abstract Class with Shape: Polymorphic Array
abstract class Shape {
    String name;
    Shape(String name) { this.name = name; }                 // constructor used through super(name)
    abstract double area();
    abstract double perimeter();
    void describe() { System.out.printf("%s area=%.2f perimeter=%.2f%n", name, area(), perimeter()); }  // concrete method uses abstract ones
}
class Circle extends Shape {
    double r;  Circle(double r) { super("Circle"); this.r = r; }
    double area() { return Math.PI * r * r; }   double perimeter() { return 2 * Math.PI * r; }
}
class Rectangle extends Shape {
    double l, b;  Rectangle(double l, double b) { super("Rectangle"); this.l = l; this.b = b; }
    double area() { return l * b; }   double perimeter() { return 2 * (l + b); }
}
Shape[] all = { new Circle(7), new Rectangle(10, 5), new Rectangle(3, 4) };
double total = 0;
for (Shape s : all) { s.describe(); total += s.area(); }     // Shape reference, each object's own formula
System.out.println("Total area = " + total);
  • Rectangle 10 × 5 → area 50, perimeter 30. A Shape reference pointing to the rectangle calls the rectangle's area()
  • Abstract class as a *type*method parameters, arrays and return types can all be Shape
  • Adding a new shape (Triangle) needs no change in the loop — this is the payoff of abstraction
Practice — Abstract Class Questions
QuestionAnswer
Can an abstract class have a constructor?✔ yes — runs when a concrete subclass object is created (super(...))
Can an abstract class have no abstract methods?✔ yes — used only to stop direct instantiation
Can it have concrete methods and fields?✔ yes
Can an abstract class be final?✘ — final = can't extend, abstract = must extend
Can an abstract method be private/static/final?✘ all three
Can an abstract class have main?✔ yes — the JVM only needs a static main
What if a subclass doesn't override all abstract methods?it must be declared abstract
Can we create a reference of an abstract class?✔ — that is how polymorphism works
Abstract class vs interface?abstract class: state + partial code, single inheritance · interface: contract, many per class
Why can't new Shape() work?some methods have no body — nothing to run
14

Interfaces

Lectures 14.1 – 14.7
What Are Interfaces?

A pure contract: a collection of abstract methods (what to do, not how). A class implements it and provides the bodies. Interfaces are used for generalization & polymorphism, and to get "multiple inheritance" in Java.

interface Test {
    int MAX = 100;                    // implicitly  public static final  → constant (must be initialised)
    void method1();                   // implicitly  public abstract
    void method2();
}
class My implements Test {            // 'implements' (NOT extends) – class is bound by the contract
    public void method1() { System.out.println("method1 of My"); }    // must be public (interface methods are public)
    public void method2() { System.out.println("method2 of My"); }
    public void method3() { System.out.println("only in My"); }
}
// Test t = new Test();               // ✘ Test is abstract; cannot be instantiated
Test t = new My();                    // ✔ interface reference → object of implementing class
t.method1();                          // My's version
// t.method3();                       // ✘ cannot find symbol – reference type Test knows only Test's methods
((My) t).method3();                   // ✔ after casting (or use  My m = new My();)
  • Keywordsinterface to define, implements to use; class My extends Test ✘ error (*no interface expected here*)
  • If class doesn't implement everythingdeclare it abstract (otherwise compile error)
  • Same as abstract class?purpose is polymorphism, similar to an abstract class — but it's an interface so a class can implement many
  • Reference type decides the visible methodsonly members of the interface are accessible via an interface reference
Example — SmartPhone with Phone, Camera & MusicPlayer

A class can extend only one class but implement many interfaces. The reference type limits what you can use — like lending your phone to a friend *only to play music*.

interface Phone       { void call();  void sms(); }
interface Camera      { void click(); void record(); }
interface MusicPlayer { void play();  void stop(); }

class SmartPhone implements Phone, Camera, MusicPlayer {      // many interfaces
    @Override public void call()   { System.out.println("Calling"); }
    @Override public void sms()    { System.out.println("SMS sent"); }
    @Override public void click()  { System.out.println("Photo clicked"); }
    @Override public void record() { System.out.println("Recording"); }
    @Override public void play()   { System.out.println("Playing music"); }
    @Override public void stop()   { System.out.println("Stopped"); }
}
public class Demo {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Phone p = new SmartPhone();   p.call(); p.sms();          // only call & sms available
        Camera c = new SmartPhone();  c.click(); c.record();      // only camera methods
        MusicPlayer m = new SmartPhone();  m.play();              // only music methods
        // p.click();                 // ✘ cannot find symbol
    }
}
  • Real lifeholding the smartphone as a Phone — you can only call/SMS; treat it as Camera — only click/record
  • One object, many roles(polymorphism through interfaces)
Class + interfaces togetherclass Smart extends Device implements Camera, MusicPlayer { }   // extends first, then implements
interface Multi extends Phone, Camera { }                       // interface can extend MANY interfaces
Student Challenge 14.5 — Callback Methods

The most common use of interfaces in Java: callback methods — you hand over an object of a class that must have a certain method, so *someone else* can call it back later.

A store keeps an array of 100 members. A customer can register only if the class implements the Member interface (which has one method callBack). The registration code stores Member references and later calls callBack() on each (e.g. to send an offer).
interface Member { void callBack(String message); }              // the contract for anybody who wants to be a member

class Customer implements Member {
    private String name;
    Customer(String n) { name = n; }
    @Override public void callBack(String msg) {                  // callback method – called by the store
        System.out.println("Hello " + name + ", " + msg);
    }
}
class Store {
    private Member[] members = new Member[100];
    private int count;
    void register(Member m) { members[count++] = m; }              // accepts ANY class that implements Member
    void announce(String offer) {
        for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) members[i].callBack(offer);   // "calling back" every member
    }
}
public class Test {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Store s = new Store();
        s.register(new Customer("Ravi"));
        s.register(new Customer("Anu"));
        s.announce("50% off today");
    }
}
Event listeners (button click in AWT/Swing/JavaFX), comparators, Runnable threads, notifications, plug-in systems — the framework calls your method through an interface.
Dos and Don'ts of Interfaces
PointDetail
Fieldsimplicitly public static final → constants; must be initialised
Methodsimplicitly public abstract; no body (older Java)
Object creation✘ cannot instantiate; ✔ references allowed
Implementingclass must implement all abstract methods (as public) or be abstract
default methods (Java 8)interface method with a body — implementing classes inherit it, may override it
static methods (Java 8)called as InterfaceName.method(); not inherited
private methods (Java 9)helper methods, usable only inside default/static methods of the interface
Constructors / instance variables✘ none — that's why a default method is useful: it adds behaviour without state
Extendinginterface extends interface(s) (many allowed); class implements interface(s)
Class ⟶ Interfaceclass can extend one class and implement many interfaces
Diamond conflictif two interfaces give the same default method, the class must override it
interface Test {
    int X = 10;                              // public static final
    void method1();                          // public abstract
    default void method3() { helper(); System.out.println("method3 of Test"); }   // has body
    static void util()    { System.out.println("static in interface"); }
    private void helper() { System.out.println("private helper"); }
}
class My implements Test { public void method1() { } }
new My().method3();            // method3 of Test  – available without overriding
Test.util();                   // called through the interface name
// Test.X = 20;                // ✘ cannot assign a value to final variable
Why default methods? Add a new method to an interface that thousands of classes already implement without breaking them (that's how Java added forEach, streams etc. to Collections).
Interface vs Abstract Class vs Multiple Inheritance
Abstract classInterface
Keywordabstract class / extendsinterface / implements
Methodsabstract + concreteabstract, default, static, private
Variablesany kindonly constants (public static final)
Constructors✔✘
A class can useonemany
Use forclosely related classes, sharing code/state ("is-a")a capability/role any class can add ("can-do") — generalization
  • Multiple inheritance (C++)a class inherits from many classes (Phone, Camera, MusicPlayer) → ambiguity ("diamond problem") if both parents have the same method
  • Java's answera class can extend only ONE class (you cannot extends A, B) but can implement any number of interfaces — the *smartphone* example: inherits from Phone class and implements Camera & MusicPlayer interfaces
  • Real multiple inheritance?what people call multiple inheritance in Java is really interfaces — no state inherited, so no ambiguity
  • Middle pathabstract class is *in between* a concrete class and an interface
class SmartPhone extends Phone implements Camera, MusicPlayer { }   // one class + many interfaces
Deep Dive — Interface Conventions & the Phone/Camera/Player Model

Naming habit: prefix interfaces with I — ICamera, IMusicPlayer — because a *Camera* could also be a real class (a DSLR) while the *camera capability* of a phone or a spy pen is an interface. Likewise a *MusicPlayer* device on the market vs the music-playing ability built into a car.

class Phone { void call() { print("calling"); }  void sms() { print("sms"); } }          // concrete class (methods assumed public)
interface ICamera      { void click();  void record(); }
interface IMusicPlayer { void play();   void stop(); }

class SmartPhone extends Phone implements ICamera, IMusicPlayer {      // ONE class + MANY interfaces
    public void click()  { print("photo"); }   public void record() { print("recording"); }
    public void play()   { print("music"); }   public void stop()   { print("stopped"); }
}
Phone p = new SmartPhone();       p.call(); p.sms();          // as a PHONE: only call & sms
ICamera c = new SmartPhone();     c.click(); c.record();      // as a CAMERA: only click & record
IMusicPlayer m = new SmartPhone(); m.play();                  // friend borrows it only to play music – you don't allow games
// p.click();   ✘  cannot find symbol
  • Reference = the *role*one object (the smartphone) can be treated as different types; the reference restricts what the holder can do
  • Interface methods in the implementing class must be public (interface methods are public; you can't weaken access)
  • class My implements Test without implementing all methods → write abstract class My (then a subclass finishes the job)
  • Interface cannot be extended by a class — you implement it; interfaces extend interfaces
  • Direct or indirectwhichever class ends up being concrete must implement all methods of every interface in its chain
Multiple inheritance, real-life: Amphibianclass Terrestrial { void walk() { } }                     // most amphibians are mainly land animals…
interface IAquatic { void swim(); }                        // …but can also live in water
class Amphibian extends Terrestrial implements IAquatic { public void swim() { } }
Deep Dive — default, static & private Methods (Java 8/9)
interface Test {
    void method1();                                     // abstract (public by default)
    void method2();
    default void method3() { helper(); System.out.println("method3 of Test"); }   // has a body → existing classes get it "for free"
    static  void util()    { System.out.println("static in interface"); }        // called as Test.util()
    private void helper()  { System.out.println("private helper"); }             // only usable inside default/static methods
}
class My implements Test {
    public void method1() { }   public void method2() { }        // must define the two abstract ones
    // method3 is inherited – may be overridden
}
new My().method3();          // ✔ prints: private helper, then method3 of Test
// new My().helper();        // ✘ cannot find symbol – private
  • Why default methods?an interface cannot have properties, yet you may need to add a method to an interface that hundreds of classes already implement — existing classes are not disturbed, they receive the default; new classes can override
  • Errors you'll see*interface abstract methods cannot have body* (forgot default) · *cannot find symbol* (calling the private one)
  • Private methodshold common logic shared by default methods; can't be abstract
  • Two interfaces with the same default → conflict; resolve by overriding in the class: A.super.hello();
Diamond conflictinterface A { default void hello() { print("A"); } }
interface B { default void hello() { print("B"); } }
class C implements A, B { public void hello() { A.super.hello(); B.super.hello(); } }   // must override
Deep Dive — Interfaces in Real Java: Marker, Functional & Comparators
KindWhat it isExamples
Marker interfaceno methods — just "tags" a class with a capabilitySerializable, Cloneable, RandomAccess
Functional interfaceexactly one abstract method → lambda-friendlyRunnable, Comparator, ActionListener, Predicate
Callback interfaceyou give an object; the library calls your methodMember.callBack(), listeners, Comparable
Constant holder (avoid)interface used only for constantsold style
Polymorphism with an interface typeinterface Payment { void pay(double amount); }
class Card  implements Payment { public void pay(double a) { print("card " + a); } }
class Upi   implements Payment { public void pay(double a) { print("upi "  + a); } }
class Wallet implements Payment { public void pay(double a) { print("wallet " + a); } }

static void checkout(Payment p, double total) { p.pay(total); }      // works with ANY implementation
checkout(new Card(), 500);   checkout(new Upi(), 250);
List<Payment> options = List.of(new Card(), new Upi(), new Wallet());
Payment gateways, plugin systems, database drivers (java.sql.Connection is an interface — each vendor supplies the implementation), collection types (List interface → ArrayList/LinkedList), dependency injection in enterprise apps.
Design choicePrefer
Unrelated classes must share a capabilityinterface (Comparable, Runnable)
Related classes share code/state + a contractabstract class
You need to inherit from an existing class alreadyinterface (only one extends is allowed)
You may later add methods without breaking implementorsinterface with default methods
Practice — Interface Questions
QuestionAnswer
Default modifiers of interface methods / fields?methods public abstract; fields public static final
Can we create an object of an interface?✘; a reference ✔ (holding an implementing class object)
class A extends Interface1?✘ error — use implements
Can an interface extend another interface?✔ — even several (interface C extends A, B)
Can a class implement two interfaces with the same method?✔ — one implementation satisfies both
What if a class doesn't implement all interface methods?make it abstract
Since Java 8 what can interfaces hold?default & static methods; Java 9: private methods
Is multiple inheritance supported?not of classes; through interfaces ✔ (class + many interfaces)
Why don't interfaces have constructors?no state to initialise
What is a callback?you pass an object implementing an interface; the other side calls back a method when something happens
15

Inner Classes (Nested, Local, Anonymous, Static)

Lectures 15.1 – 15.5
What Are Inner Classes?

A class defined inside another class. Its purpose is limited to the outer class — a helper that nobody else needs.

KindWhere declaredNeeds outer object?Sees outer's members
Nested (member) inner classinside class body, outside methods✔ yesall (even private, static & non-static)
Local inner classinside a method (or block)✔ (in an instance method)all + effectively-final local variables
Anonymous inner classno name — declared & created in one expression✔same as local
Static nested classinside class with static✘ noonly static members of outer
  • Access ruleinner class can access outer members directly; outer class cannot access inner's members directly — it must create an inner object first
  • Benefitslogical grouping, encapsulation (hide helper class), more readable code, event handlers
  • Compiled namesOuter$Inner.class (separate class file for every inner class)
Nested Inner Class (Member Class)
class Outer {
    private int x = 10;
    void display() { Inner in = new Inner(); in.show(); }   // outer must create the inner object

    class Inner {                                           // nested inner class
        int y = 20;
        void show() { System.out.println(x + " " + y); }    // x directly accessible (even though private)
    }
}
public class Test {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Outer o = new Outer();                 // 1) object of OUTER first
        Outer.Inner i = o.new Inner();         // 2) then object of inner through it:  outer.new Inner()
        i.show();                              // 10 20
        // Inner i2 = new Inner();             // ✘ cannot find symbol – inner is not visible without Outer
        // System.out.println(o.y);            // ✘ outer can't see y directly
    }
}
  • Refer with outer nameOuter.Inner — you cannot use the inner class alone; without an outer object an inner object is of no use
  • Name clashif inner has x too, use Outer.this.x for the outer's x
  • Error you may see*cannot find symbol: variable y, location: class Outer* — outer cannot use inner's variable directly
Inner class objects are created inside the outer class in most real programs (new Inner() from an outer method).
Local & Anonymous Inner Classes

Local inner class = class defined inside a method; visible only in that method. Useful when you need a class that extends a class / implements an interface and is needed only there.

Local inner classclass Outer {
    void display() {
        class Inner {                          // local to display() – invisible outside
            void innerDisplay() { System.out.println("Inside local class"); }
        }
        Inner i = new Inner();
        i.innerDisplay();
    }
}

Anonymous inner class = no name; you create a subclass / implementation and its object in one shot — used once. Works for an abstract class, a class, or an interface (which cannot be instantiated normally).

Anonymousabstract class Greeting { abstract void hello(); }
interface Action { void perform(); }

Greeting g = new Greeting() {                      // creates an unnamed subclass of the abstract class
    void hello() { System.out.println("Hello!"); }
};
g.hello();

Action a = new Action() {                          // unnamed class implementing the interface
    public void perform() { System.out.println("Performing"); }
};
a.perform();

new Thread(new Runnable() { public void run() { System.out.println("in thread"); } }).start();
  • Ends with ;it is an expression/statement
  • Cannot have constructors(no name) — but can have initialiser blocks
  • Local variables used inside must be final / effectively final
One-off event handlers (button click), Runnable for a thread, comparators — replaced by lambda expressions (see Lambda section) when the interface has a single method.
Static Inner Class

A static class inside a class — it is a static member of the outer class. No outer object needed.

class Outer {
    static int a = 5;
    int b = 6;
    static class Inner {
        void show() {
            System.out.println(a);          // ✔ static member of outer
            // System.out.println(b);       // ✘ non-static member – needs an Outer object
        }
    }
}
Outer.Inner in = new Outer.Inner();         // created directly, outside Outer, no Outer object
in.show();
Nested (non-static)Static
Dependent on outer object✔✘
Creationouter.new Inner()new Outer.Inner()
Access outer membersallonly static ones
Can have static members✘ (before Java 16)✔
  • Only inner classes can be statica top-level class can't be declared static (*modifier static not allowed here*)
  • Inside a static context you can access only static members — same rule as static methods
Deep Dive — Inner Class Access Rules & Compiled Files
  • Inner can use outer, outer needs an objectan inner class sees all members of the outer class (even private) with no object; the outer class can reach inner members only through an inner object
  • Interfaces tooyou can nest an interface inside a class as well as a class inside a class/interface
  • Outer's name clashOuter.this.x = outer's x when inner has its own x
class Outer {
    private int x = 10;
    class Inner {
        int x = 20;                                   // same name as outer's x
        int y = 30;
        void show() {
            int x = 5;
            System.out.println(x);                    // 5  – local variable
            System.out.println(this.x);               // 20 – Inner's own field
            System.out.println(Outer.this.x);         // 10 – outer's field (even though private)
        }
    }
    void outerDisplay() {
        Inner i = new Inner();                        // outer creates an inner object to use inner members
        System.out.println(i.y);                      // y is NOT directly available; needs the object i
    }
}
Outer o = new Outer();
Outer.Inner in = o.new Inner();                       // OUTER object first, then the inner one
in.show();
KindClass file produced
Top-levelOuter.class
Nested innerOuter$Inner.class — *totally associated with the outer class*
Local inner (in method)Outer$1Local.class
AnonymousOuter$1.class, Outer$2.class
Static nestedOuter$Nested.class
Error you will meet: *cannot find symbol: variable y, location: class Outer* — the outer class tried to use an inner member directly. Create an inner object (new Inner().y) first.
  • Inner class modifierscan be default, private, protected, public, static, final, abstract — while an outer (top-level) class can be only default or public
Deep Dive — When to Use Which Inner Class
NeedUseReason
A helper class used only by one outer class and needs its datamember inner classlogical grouping, direct access to outer fields — e.g. Node inside LinkedList, Iterator implementation
Helper needed only inside one method, maybe extends a class / implements an interfacelocal inner classinvisible elsewhere
One-off implementation of an interface / abstract class (listener, Runnable, Comparator)anonymous inner classno separate named class file to maintain
Helper that doesn't need an outer *object* (utility group, builder)static nested classno hidden reference to outer
One method interface, tiny codelambdashorter than anonymous class
Anonymous class with an abstract class — "no name, object created on the fly"abstract class Greeting { abstract void hello(); }

Greeting g = new Greeting() {                     // ends with ; – it is an expression
    void hello() { System.out.println("Hello from anonymous"); }
};
g.hello();

Runnable r = new Runnable() { public void run() { System.out.println("run"); } };   // interface → object of an anonymous class
new Object() { void hi() { System.out.println("hi"); } }.hi();                        // an object used for one line only, no reference
Iterator as an inner class (classic use)class NumberList implements Iterable<Integer> {
    private int[] data = {5, 3, 8};
    public java.util.Iterator<Integer> iterator() {
        return new java.util.Iterator<>() {              // anonymous inner class uses outer's private 'data'
            int i = 0;
            public boolean hasNext() { return i < data.length; }
            public Integer next()    { return data[i++]; }
        };
    }
}
for (int n : new NumberList()) System.out.println(n);
  • Static context ruleinside a static inner class you can access only static members of the outer class — *non-static variable x cannot be referenced from a static context*
  • Objects created only for one linenew Outer().new Inner().show();
  • Memory notea non-static inner object keeps a hidden reference to its outer object — can prevent garbage collection if the inner object lives long
  • Local variables captured by a local/anonymous class must be final / effectively final
Practice — Inner Class Questions
QuestionAnswer
Can outer access inner's members directly?✘ — needs an inner object; inner can access outer directly
How to create an inner object from main?Outer.Inner i = new Outer().new Inner();
How to create a static inner object?new Outer.Inner(); — no outer object
Can a top-level class be static?✘ — *modifier static not allowed here*; only nested classes
Where is a local class visible?only inside the method (or block) that declares it
Why anonymous classes?implement/extend a type for a single use without naming a class
Can an anonymous class have a constructor?✘ (no name) — use an initializer block

16

static, final, Static Blocks & Singleton

Lectures 16.1 – 16.8
static Keyword — Uses

static = belongs to the class, not to any object ("metadata" of the class). Used for: static variables · static methods · static blocks · static nested classes. Loaded once when the class loads (Method Area).

Static variable – shared by all objectsclass House {
    static double price = 10;          // ONE copy for the whole class
    int rooms;                          // each object has its own copy
}
House h1 = new House(), h2 = new House();
System.out.println(h1.price + " " + h2.price);   // 10.0 10.0
h1.price = 20;                                    // change through any object…
System.out.println(h2.price);                     // 20.0  ← visible to all
System.out.println(House.price);                  // best practice: access with CLASS NAME
Static methodclass T {
    static int x = 100;   int y = 20;
    static void show() {
        System.out.println(x);            // ✔
        // System.out.println(y);         // ✘ non-static variable y cannot be referenced from a static context
        // this.y / non-static method call  ✘
    }
    void normal() { show(); System.out.println(y); }    // instance method can use both
}
T.show();                                 // call using class name – no object needed (like Math.sqrt())
static memberinstance member
Belongs toclasseach object
Copiesone (shared)one per object
AccessClassName.memberobject.member
static method can useonly static members; no this/supereverything
MemoryMethod area, at class loadHeap, at object creation
  • Why main is staticJVM calls it without creating an object
  • Shared datacounters, common settings, constants
  • Where allowedvariables, methods, blocks, nested classes — not a top-level class
Count objects created, keep a common interest rate for all accounts, utility methods (Math, Integer.parseInt), constants.
Static Block

A block with static { … } executed once when the class is loaded — before any object and before main. Used to initialise/setup static data.

public class Demo {
    static int x;
    static { System.out.println("Block 1"); x = 10; }
    static { System.out.println("Block 2"); }        // executes in written order
    public static void main(String[] args) { System.out.println("main"); }
}
OutputBlock 1
Block 2
main
  • In another classa class with only static blocks runs them when the class is first used (e.g. new Test(); in main → prints *main* first, then Block 1, Block 2)
  • Can accessonly static members
  • Multiple blocksallowed — executed top to bottom
  • Class loading (JVM)static blocks run in the initialization phase of class loading
Instance initialiser block { … } (no static) runs before the constructor each time an object is created.
final Keyword — Variable, Method, Class
final onMeaningError if violated
variablevalue can be assigned once – a constant*cannot assign a value to final variable*
methodcannot be overridden in subclass*overridden method is final*
classcannot be extended (e.g. String, Integer, Math)*cannot inherit from final*
parameter / localcannot be reassigned inside
reference variablethe reference can't change; the object's contents still can
class Circle {
    final double PI = 3.14159;                 // must be initialised …
    final int min;                             // … at declaration, or in EVERY constructor, or an initialiser block
    static final double E = 2.718;             // typical constant: static final, UPPER_CASE
    Circle()          { min = 0; }             // "blank final" initialised in constructor
    Circle(int m)     { min = m; }             // must be done in every constructor (don't know which is called)
    void f() { /* min = 5; ✘ final variable cannot be modified */ }
}
class Super { final void method1() { System.out.println("hello"); } }
class Sub extends Super { /* void method1() {} ✘ cannot override final method */ }
final class Locked { }
/* class X extends Locked {}  ✘ cannot inherit from final Locked */
A final field left uninitialised as a class member is a compile error (*variable pi might not have been initialized*). For a local final variable you only get the error when you use it before assigning.
final int[] a = {1,2}; a[0] = 9; ✔ allowed (object contents change) — but a = new int[3]; ✘.
final ≠ static. *static* = one copy shared by the class; *final* = cannot change. Constants are usually static final. Combined rules: abstract × final ✘, abstract × static (method) ✘.
Singleton Class

A class that can have only ONE object. Every user shares that same object.

class Singleton {
    private static Singleton instance;                 // the only object (static → belongs to class)
    private Singleton() { System.out.println("Object created"); }   // private constructor: no outside 'new'

    public static Singleton getInstance() {            // static because there is no object to call it on
        if (instance == null)                          // create only the first time
            instance = new Singleton();
        return instance;                               // afterwards just return the reference
    }
}
public class Test {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Singleton s = new Singleton();              // ✘ constructor has private access
        Singleton a = Singleton.getInstance();         // creates
        Singleton b = Singleton.getInstance();         // returns same
        System.out.println(a == b);                    // true
    }
}
  • Stepsprivate constructor → private static reference → public static getInstance() with null check
  • Limit to N objectskeep a static counter — allow creation while count < 5, else return an existing one
  • Real-life usesdatabase connection, logger, configuration, printer spooler, cache
  • Thread-safetyin multithreaded code make getInstance() synchronized (see Multithreading)
Student Challenge 16.8 — Auto-generated Student Roll Number
Roll numbers were passed from main in the earlier Student class. Make the class generate them itself: a static counter shared by all objects increments for every new student.
class Student {
    private static int count = 0;          // shared by ALL students
    private final int rollNo;              // read-only – no setter
    private String name;

    Student(String name) {
        this.name = name;
        rollNo = ++count;                  // 1, 2, 3 …
        // or include the year: rollNo = java.time.Year.now().getValue() * 1000 + (++count);
    }
    int getRollNo() { return rollNo; }
    static int getCount() { return count; }         // static method → uses only static data
}
Student s1 = new Student("Ravi");    // roll 1
Student s2 = new Student("Anu");     // roll 2
System.out.println(s2.getRollNo() + " of " + Student.getCount());
java.util.Date.getYear() is deprecated (marked obsolete by the compiler) — use java.time (see Date & Time) instead.
Deep Dive — static = "Metadata" (Data About the Class)

static is used for metadata — data about data. If some information belongs to the class as a whole, not to any particular object, make it static. Static members live in the Method Area, one copy for all objects.

Honda City example from the lectureclass HondaCity {
    static double price = 1_000_000;                    // shared by every Honda City ("price of the model")
    int registrationNo;                                 // each car has its own
    static double onRoadPrice(String city) {            // info about the CLASS – no car needed
        return price * 1.10;                             // price + 10 % (taxes) – only static members used
    }
}
System.out.println(HondaCity.onRoadPrice("Delhi"));     // ask the showroom for the price – you don't buy the car first!
HondaCity h1 = new HondaCity(), h2 = new HondaCity();
h1.price = 20;                                           // change through one object …
System.out.println(h2.price);                            // … everyone sees 20.0
  • Static variablecreated when the class is loaded, one copy, shared; access as ClassName.var
  • Static methodcalled with the class name; can use only static variables/methods; no this / super
  • Static nested classa *collection of static utilities* can be kept as a static nested class; it too can touch only static members of the outer class
  • Modifier rule*modifier static not allowed here* — only inner (nested) classes can be static, not top-level
  • Shared statea static variable is a global — great for counters/config, risky in multithreaded code
Deep Dive — When Does a Class Load? Order of Initialization

Static blocks run when the class is loaded (initialised), before any object. A class is loaded the first time you create an object, call a static method, or access a (non-constant) static field — even new Test(); in main loads Test.

class Test { static { System.out.println("Test static block 1"); }   static { System.out.println("Test static block 2"); } }
public class Main {
    static { System.out.println("Main static block"); }
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("main starts");
        new Test();                              // loading Test → its static blocks run now, in written order
    }
}
OutputMain static block
main starts
Test static block 1
Test static block 2
Full order with inheritanceclass Parent {
    static { System.out.println("1 Parent static"); }
    { System.out.println("3 Parent instance block"); }
    Parent() { System.out.println("4 Parent constructor"); }
}
class Child extends Parent {
    static { System.out.println("2 Child static"); }
    { System.out.println("5 Child instance block"); }
    Child() { System.out.println("6 Child constructor"); }
}
new Child();
new Child();       // second object: only 3-4-5-6 again (static blocks run once)
  • Orderparent static → child static → parent instance block → parent constructor → child instance block → child constructor
  • Static block can access only static members; use it to initialise/set up static data (load a driver, fill a lookup table)
  • JVM linkthis is the *Initialization* step of class loading (see JVM architecture): static variables get real values, static blocks run
Static importimport static java.lang.Math.*;
double r = sqrt(16) + pow(2, 3) + PI;      // no Math. prefix
Deep Dive — final in Every Form
final applied toRulesTypical error message
local variableassign once (may be after declaration); read only after assignment*variable might not have been initialized*
instance variablemust be initialised at declaration, in an initialiser block, or in EVERY constructor (JVM doesn't know which constructor is called)*variable might not have been initialized*
static finalcompile-time constant; initialise at declaration or in a static block; UPPER_CASE*cannot assign a value to final variable*
parametercan't be reassigned in the method
reference variablereference fixed; object's contents can still change (final int[] a; a[0]=5 ✔)
