class Circle {
    final double PI = 3.14159;        // constant per object
    final int min;                    // blank final
    Circle()        { min = 0; }      // every constructor must assign it
    Circle(int m)   { min = m; }
    void test() { /* min = 5;  ✘ cannot assign a value to final variable min */ }
}
final class Locked { }                // class Extra extends Locked { }  ✘
class Super { final void method1() { System.out.println("hello"); } }
class Sub extends Super { /* void method1() {}  ✘ */ void method2() { } }   // inherits method1, can't redefine it
staticfinal
one copy for the classcan't be changed / overridden / extended
about sharingabout immutability

Deep Dive — Singleton: Coffee Machine, N-Instances & Thread Safety

Real-life feel: a coffee machine in a small office (5–10 people) — everybody must use the same machine; creating a second machine would waste resources and split the stock.

Steps: private constructor → private static reference → public static getInstance()class CoffeeMachine {
    private int coffeeQty;
    private static CoffeeMachine instance;                    // the ONE object, created lazily
    private CoffeeMachine() { coffeeQty = 100; }              // "default constructor fills initial quantity" – but PRIVATE
    public static CoffeeMachine getInstance() {               // static: no object exists yet to call it on
        if (instance == null) instance = new CoffeeMachine(); // only the FIRST call creates
        return instance;                                       // later calls return the same reference
    }
    public boolean serve() { if (coffeeQty == 0) return false; coffeeQty--; return true; }
}
// CoffeeMachine c = new CoffeeMachine();   ✘ constructor has private access
CoffeeMachine a = CoffeeMachine.getInstance(), b = CoffeeMachine.getInstance();
System.out.println(a == b);          // true
Challenge from the lecture: allow only five objects. Add one more static property (a counter) and reuse the same idea.
class Limited {
    private static final int MAX = 5;
    private static final Limited[] pool = new Limited[MAX];
    private static int created = 0, next = 0;
    private Limited() { }
    public static Limited getInstance() {
        if (created < MAX) pool[created++] = new Limited();      // create until the limit is reached
        Limited l = pool[next];  next = (next + 1) % created;    // then hand out the existing ones in turn
        return l;
    }
}
Thread-safe versionsclass S1 { private static final S1 INSTANCE = new S1();  private S1() {}  public static S1 get() { return INSTANCE; } }   // eager
class S2 { private static S2 i;  private S2() {}  public static synchronized S2 get() { if (i == null) i = new S2(); return i; } }   // lazy + synchronized
class S3 { private S3() {}  private static class Holder { static final S3 I = new S3(); }  public static S3 get() { return Holder.I; } }   // lazy holder – uses class loading (a static nested class!)
enum S4 { INSTANCE; public void hello() { } }                          // enum singleton – safest (also survives serialization / reflection)
Logger, application configuration, database connection pool manager, cache, printer spooler, device drivers.
Practice — static / final / Singleton Questions
QuestionAnswer
Why is main static?JVM calls it without creating an object
Can a static method use an instance variable?✘ *non-static variable cannot be referenced from a static context* — unless you have an object
Can we override a static method?✘ (hidden)
When does a static block run?once, when the class is loaded, before objects and before main (for the main class)
Where are static variables stored?method area, one copy per class
Can a constructor be static / final?✘ both
Difference between final, finally, finalize?modifier · block after try/catch · GC hook method
How to make a class immutable?final class, private final fields, no setters, deep-copy mutable inputs
Singleton — how to block new?private constructor + static getInstance()
Is static final int X = 10; allowed to be changed?✘ constant
Student roll number challenge (16.8): use a private static int count in Student; the constructor does rollNo = ++count. The roll number is read-only (no setter) and main no longer passes it — the class generates it. Try adding the year: year * 1000 + ++count.
17

Packages & Access Modifiers

Lectures 17.1 – 17.5
What Are Packages?

A package is a folder that groups related classes and interfaces (namespace). Java's library itself is organised in packages: java.lang (String, Math, Exception, Runnable…), java.util (Scanner, ArrayList…), java.io, java.sql, …

  • Class filesthe compiled .class files are kept inside folders matching the package name
  • Whyorganisation, avoid name clashes (two classes named Student in different packages), access control, reuse
  • importmakes classes of a package visible without writing the full name
import java.util.Scanner;        // one class
import java.util.*;              // all classes of java.util (not sub-packages)
import java.lang.Runnable;       // java.lang.* is imported automatically; single imports are optional

java.util.Date d = new java.util.Date();   // fully-qualified name – no import needed
import static java.lang.Math.*;            // static import: sqrt(16) instead of Math.sqrt(16)
  • Only what you needif you need just String, import just that class; if you don't import a class you must use its full name
  • Import doesn't copyit only tells the compiler where to look; unused imports cost nothing at runtime
Creating Your Own Package
Demo.java  (put in your working folder)package mypack1;                    // MUST be the very first statement

public class Demo {                 // public → usable from other packages
    public void show() { System.out.println("Welcome to Demo"); }
}
Sub-package  mypack1.mypack2package mypack1.mypack2;
public class Demo3 { public void show() { System.out.println("Welcome to Demo3"); } }
Compile with -d (destination) — a dot means "current directory"javac -d . Demo.java            // creates folder mypack1/ with Demo.class inside
javac -d . Demo3.java           // creates mypack1/mypack2/Demo3.class
Using them from another classimport mypack1.Demo;
import mypack1.mypack2.Demo3;
public class Test {                 // has main – no package (or its own)
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        new Demo().show();
        new Demo3().show();
    }
}
Run a class that is inside a package: use its full namejava mypack1.Demo
  • Folder = packagepackage a.b.c; ⇒ folders a/b/c/
  • One package statement per file, before imports and classes
  • Without -d.class is created next to the source and the package folders are not built
  • Classes/members from another package must be public (or protected for subclasses)
Access Modifiers (Access Specifiers)

Control who can see a class/member. Four levels: private → *(default)* → protected → public.

ModifierSame classSame packageSubclass in different packageNon-subclass in different package
public✔✔✔✔
protected✔✔✔ (through inheritance)✘
*default* (no keyword)✔✔✘✘
private✔✘✘✘
public = everywhere
default = only within the package
protected = default + subclasses of other packages
private = only within the class
  • How to rememberlearn per modifier, not per table row. public everywhere · default within the package only · protected and private: protected is "like public" except non-subclasses in a different package cannot access it · private only inside the class
  • Outer (top-level) classcan be only public or default — never private/protected
  • Interface membersalways public
  • Default"package-private" — no keyword is written
Applies toAllowed modifiers
Top-level classpublic, default
Nested/inner class, fields, methods, constructorsall four
Local variablesnone (only final)
Demo — Effect of Access Modifiers
mypack1/Demo1.javapackage mypack1;
public class Demo1 {
    int a = 1;                  // default
    private int b = 2;
    protected int c = 3;
    public int d = 4;
    public void display() { System.out.println(a + b + c + d); }   // inside the class: ALL four usable
}
Same package (mypack1) – Demo2package mypack1;
class Demo2 {
    void test() {
        Demo1 o = new Demo1();
        System.out.println(o.a + o.c + o.d);       // ✔ default, protected, public
        // System.out.println(o.b);                // ✘ b has private access
    }
}
Other package, SUBCLASS – Demo3package mypack2;
import mypack1.Demo1;                             // import needed
class Demo3 extends Demo1 {
    void test() {
        System.out.println(c + d);                // ✔ protected (inherited) + public
        // a → ✘ not visible (default)   b → ✘ private
    }
}
Other package, NOT a subclass – Demo4package mypack2;
import mypack1.Demo1;
class Demo4 {
    void test() {
        Demo1 o = new Demo1();
        System.out.println(o.d);                  // ✔ only public
        // o.a ✘, o.b ✘, o.c ✘ (protected not allowed for non-subclass)
    }
}
The compiler doesn't say "default not allowed" — it reports *a is not public in Demo1; cannot be accessed from outside package*. Learn to read such messages.
Package Naming Convention
  • Ruleuse your organisation's domain name in reverse, all lowercase, then project/module names
  • Exampleuniversity site www.unif.com → com.unif → sub-packages by area:
com.unif.academic       ← Student, Faculty, Course, Book
com.unif.admission
com.unif.finance
package com.unif.academic;
public class Student { }
  • Folder structurecom/unif/academic/Student.java; com contains unif, which contains academic
  • Why the conventiontwo companies writing a Student class won't clash — com.abc.Student vs com.xyz.Student — and the package tells the owner and purpose
  • Reservednames starting with java. / javax. belong to the JDK
Deep Dive — Built-in Packages & Three Ways to Use a Class

Java's library is a big tree of packages (folders) full of .java classes — e.g. String.java sits in java/lang. java.lang is imported automatically, so String, Math, Runnable, ArithmeticException, AutoCloseable, Boolean … are always available.

// 1) import ALL classes of the package (star)
import java.lang.*;                 // (star = every class/interface directly inside; NOT sub-packages)
// 2) import ONLY the class/interface you need
import java.lang.String;   import java.lang.Runnable;
// 3) no import – give the full path (parent.sub-package.Class) EVERY time, on the type and on the constructor
java.lang.String str = new java.lang.String("Hello");
  • Name clashjava.util.Date and java.sql.Date share a simple name — import one, write the other fully-qualified: java.sql.Date d = new java.sql.Date(0);
  • import ≠ copyit's a compile-time shortcut for names; unused imports don't slow anything
  • Sub-packages aren't includedimport java.util.*; does not import java.util.concurrent.*
  • Classes are in .java files in a folder tree; the compiled .class files sit in the same folder structure
Deep Dive — Creating & Using Packages: Step by Step
1

Write the class and start it with the package statement

package mypack1; — the very first statement in the file, before imports and classes

2

Compile with -d

javac -d . Demo.java — -d = destination directory; . = current folder (here myjava) → folder mypack1/ is created and Demo.class placed inside. Without -d a normal compile leaves the class next to the source, not inside the package folder

3

Any destination works

javac -d C:\ Demo.java puts mypack1 under C:\ — you decide where the package tree lives

4

Sub-package

package mypack1.mypack2; then javac -d . Demo3.java → mypack1/mypack2/Demo3.class

5

Use it

in another program import mypack1.Demo; (or fully-qualified). That class must be public and its methods public

6

Run the *application class* (has main)

java Test — if Test itself is in a package use its full name java mypack1.Test from the parent folder

7

Class path

if the packages are somewhere else, tell the JVM/compiler: javac -cp C:\ Test.java and java -cp C:\;. Test (Linux/Mac use :)

Demo.javapackage mypack1;
public class Demo { public void display() { System.out.println("Welcome to Demo"); } }
Test.java  (in the working folder, NOT in a package)import mypack1.Demo;
import mypack1.mypack2.Demo3;
public class Test {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Demo d1 = new Demo();          d1.display();
        Demo3 d3 = new Demo3();        d3.display();
    }
}
Resulting foldersmyjava/
 ├─ Test.java  Test.class
 └─ mypack1/
     ├─ Demo.class  Demo2.class
     └─ mypack2/
         └─ Demo3.class
  • Each package membera class in the package that you want to use outside must be public
  • Two public classes can't share a file → one .java file per public class
Deep Dive — Access Modifiers: Remember by Modifier, Not by Table Row
ModifierRule to remember
privateonly within the same class — nowhere else
*default* (no keyword)only within the same package ("package-private")
protecteddefault plus subclasses in *other* packages (via inheritance) — like public, except non-subclasses in a different package cannot access it
publiceverywhere
Where the user code isprivatedefaultprotectedpublic
Same class✔✔✔✔
Same package (subclass or not)✘✔✔✔
Different package, subclass✘✘✔✔
Different package, non-subclass✘✘✘✔
  • Outer classonly default or public (never private/protected). *Inner* classes may use all four
  • Related termsis-a = inheritance (P2 extends P1) · has-a = an object of a class used inside another class
  • Compiler messages*a is not public in Demo1; cannot be accessed from outside package* (default) · *b has private access in Demo1* · *c has protected access in Demo1*
  • Practical layout of the demoDemo1 (members a=default, b=private, c=protected, d=public) in mypack1; Test (non-subclass, other package) sees only d; Demo4 extends Demo1 (other package) sees c and d; inside Demo1's own method all four are usable
protected in another package works only through inheritance (this.c, or a reference of the *subclass* type) — not through new Demo1().c written in the subclass.
package mypack2;
import mypack1.Demo1;
class Demo4 extends Demo1 {
    void test() {
        System.out.println(c + " " + d);        // ✔ protected + public (inherited)
        // System.out.println(new Demo1().c);   // ✘ protected access via a Demo1 reference in another package
    }
}
Deep Dive — Package Naming Convention & Why It Matters
  • Reverse the domain nameuniversity site www.unif.com → com.unif; then the module: com.unif.academic (Student, Faculty, Course, Book) · com.unif.admission · com.unif.finance
  • A bank site www.city.com → com.city.accounts, com.city.loans
  • Whythe domain name is unique in the world → your class becomes unique (com.unif.admission.Account ≠ com.city.accounts.Account); no clashes when different companies' code sit in one project
  • Lowercasepackage names are all lowercase; no spaces; folders mirror the name
  • Structure = designgroup by responsibility (model, service, dao, controller) — the layered layout used in Spring/enterprise projects
package com.unif.academic;
public class Student { }
Practice — Package & Access Questions
QuestionAnswer
Which package is imported by default?java.lang
Does import java.util.* import sub-packages?✘ — only classes directly in java.util
Can a top-level class be private?✘ — default or public only
Can two classes in different packages have the same name?✔ that is the purpose of packages
What does javac -d . do?compiles and places class files into folders that match the package statement, starting at the current directory
Is package statement mandatory?✘ (without it the class is in the unnamed *default package*)
Which access modifier is the default?package-private (no keyword)
Can a subclass in another package use a default member?✘
Can a subclass in another package use a protected member?✔ through inheritance
How to avoid name clash of Date?import one, write the other with its full package name
18

Exception Handling

Lectures 18.1 – 18.10
Types of Errors — What Is an Exception?
Error typeWhenExampleWho fixes
Syntax / compile-time errorwhile compiling — code breaks language rulesmissing ;, undeclared variableprogrammer, before running
Logical errorprogram runs but gives wrong resultwrong formula, area = l + bprogrammer (testing/debugging)
Runtime error = Exceptionwhile running — an abnormal situationdivide by zero, file not found, bad input, no networkprogrammer must handle
  • Definitionan exception is a runtime error — an event that disrupts the normal flow; without handling the program terminates abnormally at that line (remaining statements don't run)
  • Causesmostly external — wrong input from the user, missing file, network/database failure, memory — not a bug in the logic
  • Car analogythe car is perfect but there is no fuel → it stops. Whose fault? The owner's (external condition), not the manufacturer's. A program stops for the same kind of reasons
  • Goal of exception handling(1) show a proper message instead of crashing, (2) let the program continue with the rest of the work, (3) release resources
try – catch (How to Handle)
Without handlingint a = 10, b = 0;
int c = a / b;                                  // ArithmeticException: / by zero → program stops here
System.out.println("Never printed");
With handlingScanner sc = new Scanner(System.in);
try {
    System.out.print("Enter denominator: ");
    int b = sc.nextInt();
    int c = 10 / b;                             // if exception occurs → jump to catch, skip next lines of try
    System.out.println("Result = " + c);        // skipped on error
} catch (ArithmeticException e) {               // handler for that type
    System.out.println("Please give a proper denominator");
}
System.out.println("Bye");                      // executes in both cases – program did NOT crash
  • Flowexception at line N of try → remaining try statements are skipped → matching catch runs → continues after the whole try-catch. Statements already executed before the error stay executed
  • try needsat least one catch or finally
  • Exception object ecarries type, message and stack trace
Method on eGives
e.getMessage()short description (/ by zero)
e.toString()class name + message
e.printStackTrace()full trace: where it happened & who called (for developers)
e.getClass().getName()exception class name
e.getCause()the wrapped original exception
Log the stack trace / send the details to the developer so the bug can be fixed in the next version, while the user only sees a friendly message.
Multiple catch & Nested try-catch
Multiple catch – one handler per kind of failuretry {
    int[] a = new int[3];
    int x = Integer.parseInt(args[0]);
    a[x] = 100 / x;
} catch (ArithmeticException e)               { System.out.println("Division by zero"); }
  catch (ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException e)    { System.out.println("Bad index"); }
  catch (NumberFormatException e)             { System.out.println("Not a number"); }
  catch (Exception e)                         { System.out.println("Something else: " + e); }   // general one LAST
Multi-catch (Java 7) – same handling for several typestry { ... } catch (ArithmeticException | NumberFormatException e) { System.out.println(e.getMessage()); }
Nested trytry {                                   // outer
    int a = 10 / 2;
    try {                               // inner
        int b = a / 0;
    } catch (ArithmeticException e) { System.out.println("inner handled"); }
    String s = null; s.length();        // outer handles this
} catch (NullPointerException e) { System.out.println("outer handled"); }
System.out.println("by");               // proves the program ran to the end
  • Rule 1subclass exceptions first, superclass last — a catch (Exception e) before catch (ArithmeticException e) is a compile error (*exception already caught*)
  • Rule 2only one catch block runs per exception
  • Nestedif the inner block can't handle it, the exception propagates to the outer try
  • Only two-of-manyas soon as one statement fails, the following ones in the try are not executed
Exception Class Hierarchy
Object
 └─ Throwable
     ├─ Error  (serious JVM problems – don't catch: OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError, VirtualMachineError)
     └─ Exception
         ├─ IOException  (checked) ── FileNotFoundException, EOFException
         ├─ SQLException, ClassNotFoundException, InterruptedException  (checked)
         └─ RuntimeException  (UNCHECKED)
              ├─ ArithmeticException
              ├─ NullPointerException
              ├─ ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException / IndexOutOfBoundsException
              ├─ NumberFormatException  (⊂ IllegalArgumentException)
              ├─ ClassCastException, InputMismatchException, NegativeArraySizeException
ExceptionTypical cause
ArithmeticExceptioninteger / 0 or % 0
NullPointerExceptioncalling a method/field on a null reference
ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsExceptionindex < 0 or ≥ length
StringIndexOutOfBoundsException"abc".charAt(5)
NumberFormatExceptionInteger.parseInt("abc")
InputMismatchExceptionScanner nextInt() on non-number
ClassCastExceptioninvalid downcast
NegativeArraySizeExceptionnew int[-1]
FileNotFoundException, IOExceptionfile/stream problems (checked)
InterruptedExceptionThread.sleep() interrupted (checked)
StackOverflowError, OutOfMemoryErrorErrors, not Exceptions
  • Generic handlercatch (Exception e) catches all exceptions (but not Errors); Throwable catches everything
  • Java: Error vs ExceptionErrors = unrecoverable system problems; Exceptions = recoverable conditions
Checked vs Unchecked Exceptions
CheckedUnchecked
BaseException (not RuntimeException) and its subclassesRuntimeException and its subclasses (+ Error)
Compilerforces you to handle or declare (try-catch or throws)does not force
ExamplesIOException, FileNotFoundException, SQLException, InterruptedExceptionArithmeticException, NullPointerException, ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException
Detectedat compile timeat run time
Typical reasonexternal resources (file, network, DB) — cannot be avoidedprogramming mistakes / bad data
  • Why runtime exceptions are uncheckedif the compiler forced handling, you'd need try-catch around every division and array access
  • Compile error you'll meet*unreported exception FileNotFoundException; must be caught or declared to be thrown*
FileReader fr = new FileReader("data.txt");        // ✘ compile error: unreported exception FileNotFoundException
// fix 1: try { … } catch (FileNotFoundException e) { … }
// fix 2: add  throws FileNotFoundException  to the method signature
throw vs throws + User-Defined Exceptions
throwthrows
Meaningactually raises an exception objectdeclares that a method *may* pass an exception to its caller
Whereinside method bodyin the method signature
Followed byan object: throw new X("msg")class name(s): throws X, Y
Countone at a timemany, comma-separated
Checked exceptionsrequired to declare with throws (or catch)
Custom exception — must extend Exception (or RuntimeException)class NegativeDimensionException extends Exception {           // checked user-defined exception
    @Override public String toString() { return "Dimensions cannot be negative"; }   // or getMessage()
}
class Rectangle {
    private double length, breadth;
    Rectangle(double l, double b) throws NegativeDimensionException {   // caller must handle
        if (l < 0 || b < 0) throw new NegativeDimensionException();     // create object + throw
        length = l; breadth = b;
    }
}
public class Test {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try {
            Rectangle r = new Rectangle(-5, 3);
        } catch (NegativeDimensionException e) {
            System.out.println(e);          // uses overridden toString
        }
    }
}
  • Don't catch where you throwcatching in the same place makes throwing pointless — the calling method should decide what to do; the throwing method declares throws
  • Unreachable codestatements written after throw (or return) in the same block → compile error *unreachable statement*
  • Message variantclass MyEx extends Exception { MyEx(String m){ super(m); } } then getMessage()
  • Rethrow / chainthrow new RuntimeException("wrapper", e); keeps the original as the *cause*
Name custom exception classes with the word Exception at the end — StackOverflowException, InvalidAgeException.
finally Block

Always executes — whether an exception occurred or not, even when the try block has return/break/continue, or when the exception is not handled.

try {
    System.out.println("try");
    int x = 5 / 0;
} catch (ArithmeticException e) {
    System.out.println("catch");
} finally {
    System.out.println("finally – clean-up (close file, connection…)");
}
Method that only throws – no catch, finally still runsstatic void method1() throws Exception {
    try {
        throw new Exception("problem");        // statements after this in try are unreachable
    } finally {
        System.out.println("final message");   // ✔ printed, then exception moves to caller
    }
}
  • Combinationstry–catch · try–catch–finally · try–finally (no catch)
  • return in tryfinally still runs before the method returns
  • Does NOT run only ifSystem.exit() is called, JVM crashes, or the thread is killed
  • Userelease resources (files, DB connections, sockets, locks)
finalfinallyfinalize()
modifier (variable/method/class)block after try-catchmethod called by GC before an object is destroyed (deprecated)
try-with-resources (Java 7+)

A resource = something that must be closed after use (file, stream, DB connection, socket). A program that acquires but never closes a resource keeps "holding" it.

Old way – finally must close (verbose, error-prone)FileReader fr = null;
try { fr = new FileReader("a.txt"); /* use */ }
catch (IOException e) { e.printStackTrace(); }
finally { try { if (fr != null) fr.close(); } catch (IOException e) { } }
New way – resource closed AUTOMATICALLYtry (FileReader fr = new FileReader("a.txt");          // declare resources in ()
     BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(fr)) {     // several allowed, separated by ;
    System.out.println(br.readLine());
} catch (IOException e) {
    System.out.println("Error: " + e.getMessage());
}                                                      // fr & br closed here, in REVERSE order
// fr / br are NOT accessible here – their scope is only the try block
  • Requirementresource class must implement AutoCloseable (or Closeable) — its close() is called automatically
  • No finally neededcloses even if an exception occurs or return is executed
  • Orderclosed in reverse order of creation, before the catch block runs
  • Suppressed exceptionsif close() also throws, it is attached to the main exception (getSuppressed())
  • Custom resourceclass MyRes implements AutoCloseable { public void close() { System.out.println("closed"); } }
Your own AutoCloseableclass Res implements AutoCloseable {
    Res() { System.out.println("acquired"); }
    @Override public void close() { System.out.println("released"); }
}
try (Res r = new Res()) { System.out.println("working"); }   // acquired / working / released
Student Challenge 18.10 — Stack Class with User-Defined Exceptions
Write StackOverflowException and StackUnderflowException (both extends Exception) and a Stack class with push / pop. top == -1 means empty. Push into a full stack → overflow; pop from an empty stack → underflow. Note the compiler message *unreported exception… must be caught or declared to be thrown* — callers must handle it.
class StackOverflowException  extends Exception { public String toString() { return "Stack is full";  } }
class StackUnderflowException extends Exception { public String toString() { return "Stack is empty"; } }

class Stack {
    private int[] data;  private int top = -1;
    Stack(int size) { data = new int[size]; }
    void push(int x) throws StackOverflowException {
        if (top == data.length - 1) throw new StackOverflowException();
        data[++top] = x;
    }
    int pop() throws StackUnderflowException {
        if (top == -1) throw new StackUnderflowException();
        return data[top--];
    }
}
public class StackTest {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Stack s = new Stack(2);
        try {
            s.push(10); s.push(20); s.push(30);      // 3rd push fails
        } catch (StackOverflowException e) { System.out.println(e); }
        try {
            System.out.println(s.pop() + " " + s.pop() + " " + s.pop());   // 3rd pop fails
        } catch (StackUnderflowException e) { System.out.println(e); }
    }
}
OutputStack is full
20 10 Stack is empty
19

Multithreading

Lectures 19.1 – 19.7
Multiprogramming & Control Flow

Multiprogramming / multitasking = running more than one task at the same time (CPU switches quickly between them). For a single user, many tasks run simultaneously.

FormUnitExample
Multiprocessing / multitaskingseparate processes (heavy, own memory)browser + music player + editor
Multithreadingseparate threads inside one process (light, share memory)a game: graphics, sound, input threads; a browser tab loading images in parallel
  • Control flownormally a program has one flow of control starting at main and moving statement by statement to the end — like reading a book with one finger. Only one main method per program
  • Threadan independent path of execution inside a program. Every program has at least the main thread
  • Multithreaded programhas several flows running "simultaneously" (real parallelism on multi-core CPUs, time-slicing on one core)
  • Benefitsresponsiveness, better CPU use, simpler modelling of concurrent tasks
  • Costshared data problems → synchronization
Creating Threads — Thread Class & Runnable Interface
Way 1 — extend Thread (override run)class MyThread extends Thread {
    @Override public void run() {                     // code that runs in the new thread
        for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) System.out.println(getName() + " : " + i);
    }
}
public class Test {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        MyThread t = new MyThread();
        t.start();                                    // creates the thread; JVM then calls run()
        System.out.println("main continues");
    }
}
Way 2 — implement Runnable (preferred: class can still extend another class)class MyTask implements Runnable {                    // "capable of running", but cannot run by itself
    public void run() { System.out.println("Task in " + Thread.currentThread().getName()); }
}
Thread t = new Thread(new MyTask(), "worker-1");      // give the Runnable to a Thread object
t.start();

new Thread(() -> System.out.println("lambda thread")).start();    // Runnable as lambda
extends Threadimplements Runnable
Inheritanceuses up your one extendscan extend another class and implement Runnable
Startnew MyThread().start()new Thread(runnable).start()
Object sharingeach thread its own objectone Runnable object can be shared by many threads
Ruleoverride run()implement run() (else class is abstract)
  • start() vs run()start() creates a new thread which then calls run(). Calling run() directly just runs it like a normal method in the current thread — no multithreading
  • Can't restartcalling start() twice → IllegalThreadStateException
  • main is staticso inside main you can't use this as the Runnable — create an object
  • End of programJVM ends when all non-daemon threads have finished (main may finish earlier than child threads)
Thread Priorities
  • Range1 … 10 → Thread.MIN_PRIORITY = 1, Thread.NORM_PRIORITY = 5 (default), Thread.MAX_PRIORITY = 10
  • Meaningthe CPU executes one thread at a time per core; a higher-priority thread is more likely to get CPU / longer time slices — but there is no guarantee of order (OS-dependent)
  • Child inheritsa new thread gets the priority of the thread that created it (main = 5)
Thread t1 = new Thread(task, "low");   t1.setPriority(Thread.MIN_PRIORITY);
Thread t2 = new Thread(task, "high");  t2.setPriority(Thread.MAX_PRIORITY);
System.out.println(t1.getPriority());  // 1
t1.start(); t2.start();
Never rely on priorities for correctness — use synchronization/join. Priorities are only a hint.
Thread Methods & Life Cycle
Constructorsnew Thread();                         // default name Thread-0, priority 5, state NEW
new Thread("myThread");               // with name
new Thread(runnable);                 // with task
new Thread(runnable, "name");
new Thread(group, runnable, "name");  // ThreadGroup
MethodPurpose
start()begin the thread (calls run() in new thread)
run()the thread's task
getName() / setName(s)thread name
getPriority() / setPriority(n)1–10
getState()NEW, RUNNABLE, BLOCKED, WAITING, TIMED_WAITING, TERMINATED
isAlive()started and not yet finished?
static sleep(ms)current thread pauses (checked InterruptedException)
join() / join(ms)wait for that thread to finish (main waits for child)
static yield()hint: "I'm ready to wait — let others of the same/lower priority run" (like letting the person behind you at the counter take water first)
interrupt() / isInterrupted()request a sleeping/waiting thread to stop (throws InterruptedException inside it)
setDaemon(true) / isDaemon()make it a daemon (background) thread — before start()
static currentThread()reference to the running thread
static dumpStack()prints the call stack of the current thread (which method called which)
getId(), getThreadGroup()id / group
public class Demo {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {   // sleep/join throw checked exception
        Thread t = new Thread(() -> {
            for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {
                System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " " + i);
                try { Thread.sleep(500); } catch (InterruptedException e) { return; }
            }
        }, "my-thread-1");
        System.out.println(t.getName() + " " + t.getPriority() + " " + t.getState());   // my-thread-1 5 NEW
        t.start();
        t.join();                                          // main waits until t is TERMINATED
        System.out.println("main ends, t alive? " + t.isAlive());   // false
    }
}
Life cycleNEW ──start()──► RUNNABLE ⇄ (CPU scheduler) RUNNING
                     │  sleep(ms)/join(ms)/wait(ms)  → TIMED_WAITING ─┐
                     │  wait()/join()                → WAITING       ─┼─► back to RUNNABLE
                     │  waiting for a monitor lock   → BLOCKED       ─┘
                     └─ run() ends / uncaught exception ──► TERMINATED
  • Daemon threadlow-priority background service thread; JVM exits when only daemon threads remain — best example: the garbage collector. If main ends, a daemon dies even if unfinished; if main calls join() on it, the daemon continues until done
  • Interruptsets the flag / wakes a sleeping thread → it gets InterruptedException
  • Thread inside threada thread can create other threads (parent/child)
Synchronization & Monitors

Problem: threads working on shared data (reading + writing) at the same time interleave and corrupt each other — a race condition. Solution: allow only one thread at a time inside the critical code.

  • Monitorevery Java object has a monitor (lock). synchronized code first acquires the object's monitor; other threads needing the same monitor wait (BLOCKED) until it is released
  • Wayssynchronized method (locks this), synchronized block (locks any object), static synchronized (locks the Class object)
Shared object printing a message letter by letterclass MyData {
    synchronized void display(String msg) {           // only ONE thread inside at a time
        for (int i = 0; i < msg.length(); i++) {
            System.out.print(msg.charAt(i));
            try { Thread.sleep(100); } catch (InterruptedException e) { }
        }
        System.out.println();
    }
}
class MyThread1 extends Thread {
    MyData d;  MyThread1(MyData d) { this.d = d; }      // reference to the SHARED object (not a new one!)
    public void run() { d.display("Welcome"); }
}
class MyThread2 extends Thread {
    MyData d;  MyThread2(MyData d) { this.d = d; }
    public void run() { d.display("Hello World"); }
}
public class Test {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        MyData d = new MyData();                        // ONE object shared by both threads
        new MyThread1(d).start();
        new MyThread2(d).start();
    }
}
OutputWelcome
Hello World
  • Without synchronizedletters are mixed — WHeellcloome… — since both threads print in the same method simultaneously
  • With itwhichever thread enters first prints the whole word; the other waits, even if the first thread sleep()s inside (sleep does not release the monitor)
  • Only useful on a shared objectif each thread has its own MyData object there is no conflict
Synchronized block (finer-grained)void update() {
    // non-critical work…
    synchronized (this) { count++; }          // only this part is locked
}
Synchronize as little code as possible — long locks kill parallelism; wrong lock ordering can cause deadlock (T1 holds A wants B, T2 holds B wants A).
Inter-Thread Communication — wait(), notify(), notifyAll()

Synchronization only gives mutual exclusion. When threads must cooperate (one produces, the other consumes), the programmer uses wait / notify. These are methods of Object and can be called only inside synchronized code (else IllegalMonitorStateException).

MethodEffect
wait()thread releases the monitor and goes to WAITING until notified
notify()wakes one waiting thread of this object
notifyAll()wakes all waiting threads
Producer–Consumer with ONE shared object  (flag: true = producer's turn, false = consumer's turn)class MyData {
    private int value;
    private boolean flag = true;                       // producer goes first
    synchronized void set(int v) {
        while (!flag) {                                // not producer's turn → wait
            try { wait(); } catch (InterruptedException e) { }
        }
        value = v;  System.out.println("Produced " + v);
        flag = false;                                  // now consumer's turn
        notify();                                      // wake the consumer
    }
    synchronized int get() {
        while (flag) {                                 // not consumer's turn → wait
            try { wait(); } catch (InterruptedException e) { }
        }
        System.out.println("Consumed " + value);
        flag = true;                                   // producer's turn again
        notify();
        return value;
    }
}
class Producer extends Thread { MyData d; Producer(MyData d){this.d=d;}
    public void run() { for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) d.set(i); } }
class Consumer extends Thread { MyData d; Consumer(MyData d){this.d=d;}
    public void run() { for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) d.get(); } }
  • Shared objectmust be the same single object given to both threads
  • Loop, not ifcheck the condition in a while loop (spurious wake-ups)
  • Resultstrictly alternating Produced 1, Consumed 1, Produced 2, …
Producer/consumer queues, print spoolers, message passing between threads, pipeline stages.
Multithreading Problems 19.7 — ATM Machine & Teacher–Student
ATM: one ATM object, many Customer threads — only one customer at a time may use the ATM. Write class ATM with synchronized methods (withdraw, deposit, checkBalance); each Customer extends Thread gets the same ATM reference and overrides run().
class ATM {
    private int balance = 10000;
    synchronized void withdraw(String who, int amt) {
        System.out.println(who + " entered");
        if (amt <= balance) { balance -= amt; System.out.println(who + " withdrew " + amt); }
        else System.out.println(who + " insufficient funds");
        try { Thread.sleep(500); } catch (InterruptedException e) {}
        System.out.println(who + " left, balance " + balance);
    }
}
class Customer extends Thread {
    ATM atm; int amt;
    Customer(String name, ATM atm, int amt) { super(name); this.atm = atm; this.amt = amt; }
    public void run() { atm.withdraw(getName(), amt); }
}
// main:  ATM a = new ATM();  new Customer("Ravi", a, 3000).start();  new Customer("Anu", a, 5000).start();
Teacher–Student whiteboard: teacher writes one line at a time; all students must copy it before the teacher writes the next. A Whiteboard object holds line and count (students who still have to copy). Teacher waits while count != 0; students wait until a new line is available. Uses wait()/notifyAll().
class Whiteboard {
    private String line;  private int count;            // count = students yet to copy
    private final int students;
    Whiteboard(int students) { this.students = students; }

    synchronized void write(String text) throws InterruptedException {   // teacher
        while (count != 0) wait();                       // previous line not yet copied by all
        line = text;  count = students;
        System.out.println("Teacher wrote: " + text);
        notifyAll();                                     // tell students
    }
    synchronized void copy(String student) throws InterruptedException { // each student
        while (count == 0 || !isNew(student)) wait();    // nothing new to copy
        System.out.println(student + " copied: " + line);
        count--;
        if (count == 0) notifyAll();                     // last student informs the teacher
        else wait();                                     // wait for others so a student doesn't copy twice
    }
    private boolean isNew(String s) { return true; }
}
The pattern in every problem: one shared object + synchronized methods + a state variable (flag/count) + wait()/notify().
Thread-safe counter — classic race condition democlass Counter { private int c; synchronized void inc() { c++; }  int get() { return c; } }
// Two threads × 1000 increments: with synchronized → 2000 always; without → often less

java.lang Package — Object, Wrappers, StringBuilder, Math, Enum & Reflection

Lectures 20.1 – 20.6
java.lang Package & the Object Class

java.lang is the default package (imported automatically). Object is the root of every class — every class inherits its methods.

MethodWhat it doesNotes
String toString()text form of the objectdefault: ClassName@hashHex. println(obj) calls it automatically — override it
boolean equals(Object o)are two objects "equal"default: same as == (same reference only)
int hashCode()integer code of the objectoverride together with equals
Class<?> getClass()runtime class infofinal
protected Object clone()copy of objectclass must implement Cloneable
protected void finalize()called by the garbage collector before destroying an objectJava's "destructor" — deprecated; there are no destructors in Java
wait(), wait(ms), notify(), notifyAll()thread communicationfinal — cannot be overridden
class MyObject {
    int value = 100;
    @Override public String toString() { return "MyObject[value=" + value + "]"; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        if (this == o) return true;
        if (!(o instanceof MyObject)) return false;
        return value == ((MyObject) o).value;                 // compare CONTENT
    }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return Integer.hashCode(value); }
}
MyObject o1 = new MyObject(), o2 = new MyObject(), o3 = o1;
System.out.println(o1);                       // MyObject[value=100]   (toString called automatically)
System.out.println(o1 == o3);                 // true  same object
System.out.println(o1 == o2);                 // false different objects
System.out.println(o1.equals(o2));            // true after overriding (false with the default equals)
System.out.println(o1.equals(o1));            // always true (reflexive)
  • Default equalstrue only if both references hold the same object
  • Contractif a.equals(b) then a.hashCode() == b.hashCode() (HashMap/HashSet rely on this)
  • final methodsgetClass, wait, notify, notifyAll → *cannot override* (error: method is final)
  • Even without calling toStringSystem.out.println(obj) and "" + obj call obj.toString() — that's how string conversion works everywhere
Wrapper Classes

Java is not purely object-oriented — primitives aren't objects. Wrapper classes wrap a primitive value inside an object (needed by collections, generics, null, utility methods).

PrimitiveWrapperParse method
byteByteByte.parseByte(s)
shortShortShort.parseShort(s)
intIntegerInteger.parseInt(s)
longLongLong.parseLong(s)
floatFloatFloat.parseFloat(s)
doubleDoubleDouble.parseDouble(s)
charCharacter– (Character.isDigit(c) …)
booleanBooleanBoolean.parseBoolean(s)
Creating wrapper objects – valueOfInteger a = Integer.valueOf(10);              // from primitive
Integer b = Integer.valueOf("123");           // from String
Float   f = Float.valueOf("123.5");           // decimal string works
byte    bv = 15;
Byte    y  = Byte.valueOf(bv);                // ✔ needs a byte variable
// Byte z = Byte.valueOf(15);                 // ✘ 15 is an int literal, not byte
Byte    z  = Byte.valueOf("15");              // ✔ or via String
Integer c = Integer.valueOf("1010", 2);       // radix (base) → 10
Instance methodPurpose
intValue() longValue() doubleValue() byteValue()…convert to any numeric primitive
compareTo(other)0 / negative / positive

Integer.MAX_VALUE, MIN_VALUE, SIZE, BYTESlimits

Autoboxing & UnboxingInteger x = 10;              // AUTOBOXING: int → Integer  (compiler writes Integer.valueOf(10))
int y = x;                   // UNBOXING : Integer → int   (x.intValue())
Integer s = x + 5;           // unbox, add, box again
ArrayList<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>();
list.add(5);                 // autoboxing – collections only hold objects
int first = list.get(0);     // unboxing

Integer p = 127, q = 127;    System.out.println(p == q);   // true  (cached −128…127)
Integer m = 1000, n = 1000;  System.out.println(m == n);   // false! → always use equals() for wrappers
When you parse a number from a String make sure it is really a number: Integer.parseInt("abc") → NumberFormatException. NaN: Double.NaN != Double.NaN is true — a NaN is not equal to anything, even itself; use Double.isNaN(x).
Integer i = null; int j = i; → NullPointerException (unboxing null).
String vs StringBuffer vs StringBuilder
StringStringBufferStringBuilder
Mutable?immutablemutablemutable
Thread-safe✔ (immutable)✔ synchronized methods — only one thread at a time may enter append/insert…✘ not thread-safe
Speedslow for many changes (new object each time)slower than builderfastest
Since1.01.01.5
Use forconstants, keysmutable text shared between threadsmutable text in a single thread (most common)
  • Same methodsStringBuffer and StringBuilder have identical methods; the only difference is thread safety
  • Capacitydefault capacity 16 characters; grows automatically → (old×2)+2
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("Java");
sb.append(" Programming").append(2025);   // chaining → "Java Programming2025"
sb.insert(4, " Language");                // insert at index
sb.delete(0, 5);                          // remove [0,5)
sb.deleteCharAt(0);
sb.replace(0, 3, "XYZ");
sb.reverse();
System.out.println(sb.length() + " " + sb.capacity() + " " + sb.charAt(0) + " " + sb);
String s = sb.toString();                  // back to String
StringBuffer b = new StringBuffer(50);    // initial capacity 50
// loops that build strings → use StringBuilder, NOT  s = s + x  (creates a new String each iteration)
Compare content — they do NOT override equalsnew StringBuilder("a").equals(new StringBuilder("a"));   // false
sb1.toString().equals(sb2.toString());                    // true
Math Class

All methods are public static — call as Math.method().

MethodResultExample
Math.abs(x)absolute (positive) valueMath.abs(-123) → 123
Math.pow(a, b)a<sup>b</sup> (double)Math.pow(2, 10) → 1024.0
Math.sqrt(x) / Math.cbrt(x)square / cube rootMath.sqrt(144) → 12.0
Math.max(a, b) / minlarger / smallerMath.max(3, 9) → 9
Math.ceil(x)round up to wholeMath.ceil(4.1) → 5.0
Math.floor(x)round downMath.floor(4.9) → 4.0
Math.round(x)nearest (long/int)Math.round(4.5) → 5 ; Math.round(-4.5) → -4
Math.rint(x)nearest even on .5Math.rint(2.5) → 2.0
Math.random()0.0 ≤ r < 1.0(int)(Math.random()*6)+1 → dice 1-6
Math.PI, Math.Econstants3.14159…, 2.718…
Math.sin/cos/tan(rad)trig — angles in radiansMath.sin(Math.toRadians(30)) → 0.5
Math.toRadians(d) / toDegrees(r)conversion

int big = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
System.out.println(big + 1);                    // -2147483648 (silent overflow!)
// Math.addExact(big, 1);                        // throws ArithmeticException: integer overflow
System.out.println(Math.sqrt(-1));              // NaN
System.out.println(1.0 / 0);                    // Infinity (no exception for doubles)
Enum — Enumerated Data Type

A user-defined type with a fixed set of named constants. Constants are implicitly public static final; a variable of that type can hold only those values.

Basicenum Day { MON, TUE, WED, THU, FRI, SAT, SUN }          // constants in UPPER CASE by convention

Day d = Day.WED;
// Day x = "WED";      ✘  Day y = 3;   ✘   – only the declared constants
switch (d) {                                             // enums work in switch (case label without Day.)
    case SAT: case SUN: System.out.println("Weekend"); break;
    default:            System.out.println("Weekday");
}
for (Day x : Day.values()) System.out.println(x + " " + x.ordinal());   // all constants, index from 0
System.out.println(Day.valueOf("MON"));                  // String → enum (IllegalArgumentException if invalid)
System.out.println(d.name() + " " + d.compareTo(Day.MON)); // "WED" 2
Enum with fields, constructor & methodsenum Size {
    SMALL(10), MEDIUM(20), LARGE(30);                    // each constant calls the constructor
    private final int price;
    Size(int price) { this.price = price; }              // constructor must be PRIVATE (or default) – never public
    public int getPrice() { return price; }              // other methods can be public
}
System.out.println(Size.LARGE.getPrice());              // 30
  • Inheritanceevery enum implicitly extends java.lang.Enum → can't extend another class; can implement interfaces
  • Constructorprivate or package-private only; values must be passed as parameters for each constant → need a parameterised constructor
  • Membersvalues(), valueOf(s), ordinal(), name(), compareTo(); == works for comparing enums
  • Compiledto a class, so it can have fields, methods, abstract methods per constant
Days, months, directions, order status (NEW/SHIPPED/DELIVERED), traffic-light states, sizes — safer & clearer than int codes or Strings.
Reflection API — Introduction

Reflection = examining a class at run time (its constructors, fields, methods, modifiers) and even using them — like an IDE (NetBeans) that "looks inside" a class to show its members. Advanced, not used every day, but you should know it exists. Package java.lang.reflect + class java.lang.Class.

class My {
    int a; private String s;
    My() {}
    My(int a, String s) { this.a = a; this.s = s; }
    public void display(String x, String y) { System.out.println(x + y); }
}
import java.lang.reflect.*;
public class Demo {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        Class<?> c = My.class;                           // 3 ways: My.class, obj.getClass(), Class.forName("My")
        System.out.println(c.getName());                 // My
        System.out.println(c.getSuperclass().getName()); // java.lang.Object  (every class inherits Object)

        for (Constructor<?> k : c.getDeclaredConstructors()) System.out.println(k);   // non-param + 2-param
        for (Field f : c.getDeclaredFields())               System.out.println(f);
        for (Method m : c.getDeclaredMethods())             System.out.println(m);    // display(String,String)

        Object o = c.getDeclaredConstructor().newInstance();                         // create object dynamically
        Method m = c.getMethod("display", String.class, String.class);
        m.invoke(o, "Hello ", "Reflection");                                         // call method by name
    }
}
  • Class objectdescribes a class; obtained by .class, getClass() or Class.forName("pkg.Name")
  • UsesIDE auto-completion, frameworks (Spring, Hibernate, JUnit), serialization, dependency injection, plug-ins, annotation processing
  • Costslower than direct calls, bypasses compile-time checks, can break encapsulation (setAccessible(true))

Javadoc & Annotations

Lectures 21.1 – 21.3
Java Documentation (Javadoc)

Document your classes inside the source with /** … */ comments and tags; the javadoc tool generates HTML pages (like the official Java API docs). Good practice in real application development — learners often skip it, professionals don't.

/**
 * Represents a bank account.
 * <p>Supports deposit and withdraw operations.</p>
 *
 * @author  Ravi Kumar
 * @version 1.2
 * @since   2024-01-15
 * @see     java.lang.Object
 */
public class Account {
    private double balance;

    /**
     * Withdraws money from the account.
     *
     * @param amount amount to withdraw (must be positive)
     * @return the new balance
     * @throws IllegalArgumentException if amount is more than balance
     * @deprecated use {@link #withdrawSafe(double)} instead
     */
    public double withdraw(double amount) { return balance -= amount; }
}
TagMeaning
@authorwho wrote the class
@versionversion of the class (not the Java version) — a class can be upgraded and re-versioned
@sincewhen this class/version was introduced / how long it is valid
@seereference to a related class/method (e.g. the class it inherits from)
@param namedescribes a method parameter
@returndescribes the return value
@throws / @exceptionexceptions the method can throw
@deprecatedmarks as obsolete, suggests alternative
{@link Class#method}inline hyperlink
{@code x}inline code text
Generate the HTML documentationjavadoc -d docs -author -version Account.java
  • Outputdocs/index.html and a page per class, with the description, constructors, methods, parameters
  • Comment types// single line · /* */ multi-line · /** */ documentation comment (picked up by javadoc)
  • Wherebefore class, interface, field, constructor, method
  • Also useful forIDE tooltips, team understanding and future you
Built-in Annotations

Annotations = metadata attached to a class, interface, method, field, parameter… Written with @. They don't change program logic directly; they give information to the compiler, tools or runtime.

AnnotationPurpose
@Overridetells the compiler "this overrides a parent method" — error if it does not (typo, wrong signature, or trying on a static/private method)
@Deprecatedelement is obsolete; compiler warns on use. @Deprecated(since="9", forRemoval=true)
@SuppressWarnings("…")silence warnings: "unchecked", "deprecation", "rawtypes", "all"
@SafeVarargsdeclares a varargs method with generic parameters is safe; allowed only on static, final or private methods (and constructors)
@FunctionalInterfaceinterface must have exactly one abstract method → usable for lambda expressions; compile error if you add a second
@Override – why it helpsabstract class Shape { abstract void draw(); }
class Circle extends Shape {
    @Override void draw() { }        // ✔
    // @Override void drow() { }     // ✘ error: method does not override a method from its superclass
}
class Old { @Deprecated void oldWay() { } void newWay() { } }
new Old().oldWay();                           // compiles with a deprecation warning

@SuppressWarnings("deprecation")
void useOld() { new Old().oldWay(); }         // warning suppressed

@FunctionalInterface
interface Greeting { void say(String name); }     // one method → lambda allowed
// adding a second abstract method → compile error
Without @Override, if a subclass method has a slightly different name the compiler silently treats it as a new method and your override never happens.
Meta-annotations (annotations that describe annotations)@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)                       // how long it is kept
@Target({ElementType.TYPE, ElementType.METHOD})           // where it can be used
@Documented                                                // appears in Javadoc
@Inherited                                                 // applied to a class → also to its child classes
@Repeatable(Tags.class)                                    // may be written more than once on one element
@interface Tag { String value(); }
Meta-annotationMeaning
@RetentionSOURCE (only source), CLASS (in .class, default), RUNTIME (readable via reflection)
@TargetTYPE, FIELD, METHOD, PARAMETER, CONSTRUCTOR, LOCAL_VARIABLE, …
@Documentedinclude in generated Javadoc
@Inheritedsubclasses inherit the annotation from the superclass
@Repeatablecan be applied multiple times (needs a container annotation; must be declared public if used across projects)
User-Defined Annotations

Defined with @interface. Its "methods" are elements (they look like abstract methods but are not) — the user supplies their values when applying the annotation. Elements can have default values.

import java.lang.annotation.*;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)
@Target(ElementType.TYPE)
@interface ProjectInfo {
    String project();                     // mandatory (no default)
    String developer();                   // mandatory
    String date();                        // mandatory
    int    version() default 13;          // optional – default used if not given
    String[] reviewers() default {};
}

@ProjectInfo(project = "Bank", developer = "Ravi", date = "2000-06-01")            // version = 13 by default
class Account { }

@ProjectInfo(project = "Bank", developer = "Anu", date = "2024-02-10", version = 15)
class Loan { }
Reading it at run time (RUNTIME retention + reflection)ProjectInfo pi = Account.class.getAnnotation(ProjectInfo.class);
System.out.println(pi.project() + " v" + pi.version());          // Bank v13
  • Element types allowedprimitives, String, Class, enums, other annotations, arrays of these
  • Mandatory vs optionalelements without default must be given; with default they may be omitted
  • Single value() elementcan be written as @Tag("x") without value =
  • Useframeworks (Spring @Autowired, JUnit @Test, JPA @Entity) read annotations by reflection to configure behaviour
22

Lambda Expressions & Method References

Lectures 22.1 – 22.3
Functional Interface — the Foundation

A functional interface = an interface with exactly ONE abstract method (single abstract method, SAM). A lambda expression is an implementation of that one method written in short form. @FunctionalInterface makes the compiler check it.

@FunctionalInterface
interface MyLambda { void display(); }        // one method → functional
1

Step 1 — separate class

class My implements MyLambda { public void display(){…} } then MyLambda m = new My(); (a MyLambda reference can hold a My object)

2

Step 2 — inner class

put the class inside another class / method

3

Step 3 — anonymous inner class

MyLambda m = new MyLambda() { public void display() { … } }; — no name, still lots of code

4

Step 4 — lambda

MyLambda m = () -> System.out.println("Hello World"); — for a very small thing to do, why write a whole class? just write the method.

MyLambda m = () -> System.out.println("Hello World");
m.display();
  • Default/static methodsan interface can still have them — only one abstract method is allowed
  • Types with one methodRunnable, Comparator, ActionListener, Callable
Lambda Syntax, Parameters & Return
FormExample
no parameters() -> System.out.println("Hi")
one parameter (parentheses optional)s -> s.length() · (String s) -> s.length()
two parameters(a, b) -> a + b
block body(a, b) -> { int r = a + b; return r; }
expression bodyvalue of the expression is returned automatically (no return, no ;)
typesusually inferred from the interface method — s is a String automatically
interface Add      { int add(int a, int b); }
interface Greet    { void greet(String name); }
interface Checker  { boolean check(int n); }

Add a = (x, y) -> x + y;                         // returns the sum
Greet g = name -> System.out.println("Hello " + name);
Checker even = n -> n % 2 == 0;

System.out.println(a.add(10, 20));               // 30 – lambda body runs when the method is called
g.greet("Ravi");
System.out.println(even.check(8));               // true
  • Returninterface method with return type → lambda must produce a value of that type
  • void methodexpression is executed, value ignored
  • Lambda has no name/class of its own — it becomes the method implementation of the interface
Variable Capture (Lambda vs Inner Class)

A lambda can use variables of the enclosing method only if they are final or effectively final (never modified after initialisation).

int base = 10;                                    // effectively final (never changed)
Add a = (x, y) -> x + y + base;                   // ✔ captured
// base = 20;                                     // ✘ if uncommented: "local variables referenced from a lambda
                                                  //    expression must be final or effectively final"
int[] counter = {0};                              // workaround: mutate array contents
Runnable r = () -> counter[0]++;
LambdaAnonymous inner class
thisrefers to the enclosing class objectrefers to the anonymous object itself
Compiledno separate class file (invokedynamic)Outer$1.class
Can implementonly functional interfacesany interface / abstract class
Fields / statenonecan have fields & multiple methods
Local variable accessfinal / effectively finalfinal / effectively final
  • Instance & static fieldscan be modified inside a lambda — the restriction is only for local variables
Method References (::)

A shortcut for a lambda that just calls an existing method. Works with functional interfaces.

KindSyntaxEquivalent lambda
Static methodInteger::parseInts -> Integer.parseInt(s)
Instance method of a specific objectSystem.out::printlnx -> System.out.println(x)
Instance method of an arbitrary object of a typeString::compareTo , String::toUpperCase(a, b) -> a.compareTo(b) — first parameter becomes the object, the rest are arguments
ConstructorMy::new() -> new My()
interface Conv   { int convert(String s); }
interface Maker  { My make(); }
interface Comp   { int compare(String a, String b); }

Conv c = Integer::parseInt;                    // static reference
System.out.println(c.convert("123") + 1);      // 124

Maker mk = My::new;                            // constructor reference: calling make() calls My's constructor
My obj = mk.make();

Comp cmp = String::compareTo;                  // compareTo needs ONE parameter; the other is the object itself
System.out.println(cmp.compare("apple", "banana"));   // negative

List<String> names = Arrays.asList("ravi", "anu", "zoya");
names.forEach(System.out::println);
names.replaceAll(String::toUpperCase);         // [RAVI, ANU, ZOYA]
Signature matching: the method reference's parameters must fit the functional interface method's parameters.
Built-in Functional Interfaces (java.util.function) & Everyday Use
InterfaceMethodMeaningExample
Predicate<T>boolean test(T)conditions -> s.isEmpty()
Function<T,R>R apply(T)transforms -> s.length()
Consumer<T>void accept(T)use a valueSystem.out::println
Supplier<T>T get()produce a value() -> Math.random()
UnaryOperator<T>T apply(T)T → TString::trim
BinaryOperator<T>T apply(T,T)two → one(a,b) -> a+b
BiFunction<T,U,R>R apply(T,U)two inputs
Runnable / Callable<V>run() / call()tasks() -> doWork()
Comparator<T>int compare(T,T)ordering(a,b) -> a.age - b.age
Runnable r = () -> System.out.println("running");
new Thread(r).start();

List<Integer> nums = new ArrayList<>(List.of(5, 8, 2, 9, 1));
nums.sort((a, b) -> b - a);                   // descending
nums.removeIf(n -> n % 2 == 0);               // remove evens
nums.forEach(n -> System.out.print(n + " "));

Predicate<String> longWord = s -> s.length() > 4;
Function<String,Integer> len = String::length;
System.out.println(longWord.test("lambda") + " " + len.apply("Java"));    // true 4
Bonus (from the GfG reference): Streams use lambdas heavilyList<String> res = Arrays.asList("ravi", "anu", "zoya", "amit").stream()
                         .filter(s -> s.startsWith("a"))
                         .map(String::toUpperCase)
                         .sorted()
                         .collect(java.util.stream.Collectors.toList());   // [AMIT, ANU]
Event handlers in GUI code, sorting with comparators, thread tasks, callbacks, filtering collections — replaces bulky anonymous classes.
